\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}};y)}.

For example, if we set
y_{1}=\dots=y_{6}=1
, then it remains to count the number of spanning trees:

\mathrm{Sym}_{2}(G;(2v_{2}-v_{3}-v_{4});y)=12+12+2\sqrt{12\cdot 12-12\cdot 9}=36.\qed

4.
Generalization to matroids over hyperfields

We have seen that many properties of the classical Kirchhoff and Symanzik polynomials on graphs are still true for our generalization. In this section, we wish to generalize two very important properties of the classical case: the deletion contraction formula (
4
) and the partial factorization (
5
).

To generalize these formulæ, we need to define deletion and contraction for family of vectors. The natural objects to consider for contractions and deletions are matroids. We will recall the definitions and the main properties in a first subsection.

But classical matroids do not contain enough data. We have to assign some coefficients to the bases. Recently, in
[
10
]
, Baker and Bowler define matroids over hyperfields. To do so, they use Grassman-Plücker functions. These will be the main tool to achieve our generalization of these formulæ. Though we will work in the general setting of hyperfields, to avoid technicalities one might only consider the case of fields, which will be sufficient for the case of family of vectors.

4.1.
Reminder on matroids

We begin with recalling basic definitions and properties about matroids without any proof. We refer to
[
46
]
for more information.

A matroid can have many equivalent, or
cryptomorphic
, definitions. Here is a first one.

Definition 4.1
.

A
matroid

\mathfrak{M}
is a pair which consists of a
ground set

E
, which can be any finite set, and a family of
independent sets

\mathfrak{I}
, which is a subset of
\mathrm{\mathcal{P}}(E)
. We write
\mathfrak{M}=(E,\mathfrak{I})
. A matroid has to verify three axioms:

(1)

\emptyset\in\mathfrak{I}
,

(2)

(hereditary property)
\mathfrak{I}
is stable by inclusion (
J\subseteq I\in\mathfrak{I}\Rightarrow J\in\mathfrak{I}
),

(3)

(augmentation property) if
I,J\in\mathfrak{I}
and if
|J|<|I|
, then there exists
i\in I\setminus J
such that
J+i\in\mathfrak{I}
. ∎

One could think of
E
as a set of vectors generating some vector space, and
\mathfrak{I}
as the set of free subfamilies of
E
(though, there are matroids which have not such a representation).

Let
\mathfrak{M}=(E,\mathfrak{I})
be a matroid. If
I\subseteq E
, we define the
rank of
I
by

\mathrm{rk}(I):=\max_{J\in\mathrm{\mathcal{P}}(I)\cap\mathfrak{I}}|J|.

The
rank of
\mathfrak{M}
is the rank of
E
. We call the
closure of
I
the set

\mathrm{cl}(I):=\{i\in E\,\big|\,\mathrm{rk}(I\cup\{i\})=\mathrm{rk}(I)\}\subseteq E.

This is a closure operator: it is extensive, increasing and idempotent.

A
basis
is an independent set maximal for the inclusion. The set of all bases is denoted by
\mathrm{\mathcal{B}}(\mathfrak{M})
. If
l
is a nonnegative integer, the set of all independents of rank
l
is denoted by
\mathfrak{I}_{l}
. Note that we have,
\mathrm{\mathcal{B}}(\mathfrak{M})=\mathfrak{I}_{\mathrm{rk}(\mathfrak{M})}
and that, for a given ground set
E
, a matroid is characterized by its bases. The bases verify the following
exchange property
:
if
B_{1},B_{2}
are two different bases, then for any
i\in B_{1}\setminus B_{2}
, there exists
j\in B_{2}\setminus B_{1}
such that
B_{1}-i+j
(i.e.,
B_{1}\setminus\{i\}\cup\{j\}
) is a basis.

An element
e\in E
is said to be:

•

a
loop
if
\{e\}\not\in\mathfrak{I}
,

•

free
if it is in every basis, and

•

proper
otherwise.

Let
I
be a subset of
E
. One can delete
I
from
\mathfrak{M}
to get a new matroid denoted by
\mathfrak{M}\backslash I
and defined by

\mathfrak{M}\backslash I:=(E\setminus I,\mathfrak{I}\cap\mathrm{\mathcal{P}}(E\setminus I)).

We also define the restriction of
\mathfrak{M}
to
I
by
\mathfrak{M}|I:=\mathfrak{M}\backslash I^{c}
.

One can also contract
I
in
\mathfrak{M}
to get a new matroid denoted by
\mathfrak{M}/\!I
and defined by

\mathfrak{M}/\!I:=(E\setminus I,\mathfrak{I}^{\prime}),

where

\displaystyle\mathfrak{I}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\{J\subseteq E\setminus I\,\big|\,\exists B\in\mathrm{\mathcal{B}}(\mathfrak{M}|I),J\cup B\in\mathfrak{I}\}

\displaystyle=\{J\subseteq E\setminus I\,\big|\,\forall B\in\mathrm{\mathcal{B}}(\mathfrak{M}|I),J\cup B\in\mathfrak{I}\}.

Concerning the bases, we have the following characterizations:

(16)

\begin{split}\mathrm{\mathcal{B}}(\mathfrak{M}|I)=\{B\cap I\,\big|\,B\in\mathrm{\mathcal{B}}(\mathfrak{M})\text{ and $|B\cap I|$ is maximal}\},\text{ and}\\
\mathrm{\mathcal{B}}(\mathfrak{M}/\!I)=\{B\cap I^{c}\,\big|\,B\in\mathrm{\mathcal{B}}(\mathfrak{M})\text{ and $|B\cap I^{c}|$ is minimal}\}.\end{split}

Moreover, if
I\subseteq E
and
B\subseteq E
, any two of the following statements imply the third one.

(17)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\bullet\ B\cap I\in\mathrm{\mathcal{B}}(\mathfrak{M}|I),\\
\bullet\ B\cap I^{c}\in\mathrm{\mathcal{B}}(\mathfrak{M}/\!I),\\
\bullet\ B\in\mathrm{\mathcal{B}}(\mathfrak{M}).\end{cases}

From (
16
) and (
17
), we deduce the following equivalence.

(18)

B\in\mathrm{\mathcal{B}}(\mathfrak{M})\text{ and }|B\cap I|=\mathrm{rk}(I)\quad\iff\quad B\cap I\in\mathrm{\mathcal{B}}(\mathfrak{M}|I)\text{ and }B\cap I^{c}\in\mathrm{\mathcal{B}}(\mathfrak{M}/\!I).

The dual of the matroid
\mathfrak{M}
will be denoted by
\widehat{\mathfrak{M}}
and is defined by

\mathrm{\mathcal{B}}(\widehat{\mathfrak{M}})=\{B^{c}\,\big|\,B\in\mathrm{\mathcal{B}}(\mathfrak{M})\}.

This is a duality in the sense that we have
\widehat{\widehat{\mathfrak{M}}}=\mathfrak{M}
. Moreover,
\widehat{\mathfrak{M}\backslash I}=\widehat{\mathfrak{M}}/\!I
.

4.2.
Kirchhoff and Symanzik polynomials of a matroid

Let
n
be a positive integer,
E:=[n]
be a finite set and
\mathfrak{M}=(E,\mathfrak{I})
be a matroid of rank
r
.

If
u=(u_{1},\dots,u_{n})
is a family of elements of a
\mathbb{Z}
-module, we denote by
\mathfrak{M}_{u}=(E_{u},\mathfrak{I}_{u})
, with
E_{u}=[n]
and

\mathfrak{I}_{u}=\{I\subseteq E\,\big|\,\text{$u_{I}$ is free}\},

the
matroid representing the family
u
.

Here is the definition of Kirchhoff and Symanzik polynomials of a matroid.

Definition 4.2
.

The
Kirchhoff polynomial (of order
0
) of the matroid
\mathfrak{M}
with variables
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}
is defined by

\mathrm{Kir}_{0}(\mathfrak{M};x)=\sum_{I\in\mathrm{\mathcal{B}}(\mathfrak{M})}x^{I}.

The
Symanzik polynomial (of order
0
) of the matroid
\mathfrak{M}
with variables
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}
is defined by

\mathrm{Sym}_{0}(\mathfrak{M};x)=\sum_{I\in\mathrm{\mathcal{B}}(\widehat{\mathfrak{M}})}x^{I}.\qed

This definition is natural because of the following claim, whose proof is straightforward.

Claim 4.3
.

Let
u
be a family of
n
elements in
\mathbb{Z}^{p}
. Then

\displaystyle\mathrm{Kir}_{0}(u;x)=\mathrm{Kir}_{0}(\mathfrak{M}_{u};x),

\displaystyle\mathrm{Sym}_{0}(u;x)=\mathrm{Sym}_{0}(\mathfrak{M}_{u};x).

The analogous for matroids of the duality Theorem
2.13
is given by the following claim, which is a direct corollary of Claim
4.3
and of Theorem
2.13
for
k=0
.

Claim 4.4
.

Let
q
be a positive integer and
v
be a family of
n
vectors in
\mathbb{Z}^{q}
such that
v^{{}^{\intercal}}
spans
\ker(U)
, then
\mathfrak{M}_{u}=\widehat{\mathfrak{M}}_{v}
.

Next proposition shows that parameters of Symanzik polynomials correspond to a so-called
quotient
of the matroid.

If
w
is a family of elements of
\langle u\rangle
, then
\mathfrak{M}_{u\star w}
is an extension of
\mathfrak{M}_{u}
, i.e., we have
\mathfrak{M}_{u}
equals
\mathfrak{M}_{u\star w}|{[n]}
.

Proposition 4.5
.

Let
w
consist of independent elements of
\langle u\rangle
. Then

\mathrm{Sym}_{0}(u;w;x)=\mathrm{Sym}_{0}(\mathfrak{M}_{u\star w}/\![n+1\,\raisebox{0.86108pt}{.}\,\raisebox{0.86108pt}{.}\,n+l];x).

Proof.

Looking at Definition
2.17
, the left-hand side is the sum of
x^{I^{c}}
over all
I\subseteq[n]
of size
r-l
such that
u_{I}\star w
is an independent family. But such sets
I
exactly correspond to the bases of the matroid
\mathfrak{M}_{u\star w}/\![n+1\,\raisebox{0.86108pt}{.}\,\raisebox{0.86108pt}{.}\,n+l]
. Thus, Definition
4.2
ends the proof.
∎

Remark 4.6
.

In particular, if
I\subseteq[n]
is of size
r-l
, then
x^{I^{c}}
appears with a nonzero coefficient in
\mathrm{Sym}_{k}(u;w;x)
only if
I
is in
\mathfrak{I}_{u}
. Therefore, if
\mathrm{Sym}_{k}(u;w;x)=\lVert\widetilde{\mathbf{v}}^{{}^{\intercal}}\rVert_{k,x}^{k}
for some family
\widetilde{v}
, then
\mathrm{det}(\widetilde{V}_{I^{c}})\neq 0
implies that
I\in(\mathfrak{I}_{u})_{r-l}
. This implication is an equivalence in the case
l=0
.
∎

In the next subsection, we generalize the definitions to higher orders.

4.3.
Grassman-Plücker functions

In this subsection we introduce the main tool to work with orders higher than
0
. We will work with hyperfields, which are similar to fields except that the operation of addition is multivalued. We will quickly reproduce the axioms from
[
10
]
below and refer for more information and examples to the literature (see, e.g.,
[
10
,
12
]
). However, if the reader prefers, it is possible to think of
\mathbb{F}
as a field (in this case,
“
\ni 0
”
have to be replaced by
“
=0
”
).

In a hyperfield
\mathbb{F}
, the addition is a
hyperoperation
, i.e., a map
\boxplus:\mathbb{F}\times\mathbb{F}\to\mathrm{\mathcal{P}}(\mathbb{F})\setminus\{\emptyset\}
. If
A,B\subseteq\mathbb{F}
, we define

A\boxplus B:=\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}a\in A\\
b\in B\end{subarray}}a\boxplus b.

We require
\boxplus
to be associative:
(a\boxplus b)\boxplus c=a\boxplus(b\boxplus c)
for every
a,b,c\in\mathbb{F}
.

A
commutative hypergroup
is a tuple
(G,\boxplus,0)
, where
\boxplus
is a commutative and associative hyperoperation on
G
such that:

•

0\boxplus x=\{x\}
for every
x\in G
,

•

for every
x\in G
, there is a unique element
-x\in G
such that
0\in x\boxplus-x
,

•

x\in y\boxplus z
if and only if
z\in x\boxplus-y
.

A
hyperfield
is a tuple
(\mathbb{F},{}\cdot{},\boxplus,1,0)
with
0\neq 1
such that:

•

(\mathbb{F}\setminus\{0\},{}\cdot{},1)
is a commutative group,

•

(\mathbb{F},\boxplus,0)
is a commutative hypergroup,

•

0\cdot x=x\cdot 0=0
for every
x\in\mathbb{F}
,

•

a\cdot(x\boxplus y)=(a\cdot x)\boxplus(a\cdot y)
for all
a,x,y\in\mathbb{F}
.

Let
n
be a positive integer and
E:=[n]
be a finite set. A Grassman-Plücker function over
\mathbb{F}
of rank
r
is a function
\varphi:E^{r}\to\mathbb{F}
such that

•

\varphi
is not identically zero,

•

(alternating) for any
I=(i_{1},\dots,i_{r})\in E^{r}
and for any two different indices
\ell,m
,

\varphi(i_{1},\dots,i_{\ell},\dots,i_{m},\dots,i_{r})=-\varphi(i_{1},\dots,i_{m},\dots,i_{\ell},\dots,i_{r}),

and

\varphi(i_{1},\dots,i_{\ell},\dots,i_{m},\dots,i_{r})=0

if
i_{\ell}=i_{m}
,

•

(Grassman-Plücker relations) for any
I=(i_{1},\dots,i_{r}),J=(j_{1},\dots,j_{r})\in E^{r}
and any
\ell\in[r]
,

\mathop{\raisebox{-5.0pt}{\scalebox{2}{$\boxplus$}}}\limits_{m=1}^{r}\varphi(i_{1},\dots,i_{m-1},j_{\ell},i_{m+1},\dots,i_{r})\varphi(j_{1},\dots,j_{\ell-1},i_{m},j_{\ell+1},\dots,j_{r})\boxplus-\varphi(I)\varphi(J)\ni 0.

Recall that, if
I=\{i_{1},\dots,i_{l}\}\subseteq E
with
i_{1}<\dots<i_{l}
, then we also denote by
I
the
l
-tuple
(i_{1},\dots,i_{l})\in E^{l}
. We this convention, a Grassman-Plücker function
\varphi
of rank
r
induces a function on the subsets of
E
of size
r
. Moreover, since
\varphi
is alternating,
\varphi
is characterized by its value on the subsets of
E
of size
r
.

Let
\varphi
be a Grassman-Plücker function over
\mathbb{F}
of rank
r
. Then one can verify that the set

\{I\subseteq E\,\big|\,|I|=r\text{ and }\varphi(I)\neq 0\}

is the set of bases of some matroid over the ground set
E
. We call this matroid the
underlying matroid of
\varphi
and denote it by
\mathfrak{M}_{\varphi}
.

For example, if
u=(u_{1},\dots,u_{n})
is a family of elements of
\mathbb{Z}^{p}
of rank
r
, we define

\begin{array}[]{rrcl}\varphi_{u}:&E^{r}&\to&\mathbb{R},\\
&I&\mapsto&\mathrm{det}(V_{I}),\end{array}

where
V
is defined as in Equation (
7
). Then
\varphi_{u}
is a Grassman-Plücker function and its underlying matroid is
\mathfrak{M}_{u}
, as defined in the previous subsection.

Here is the main definition of this section.

Definition 4.7
.

Let
\mathbb{F}
be a hyperfield and let
\varphi
be a Grassman-Plücker function over
\mathbb{F}
. Let
\mathfrak{M}
be its underlying matroid. Then we define

\displaystyle\mathrm{Kir}_{k}(\varphi;x)

\displaystyle:=\sum_{I\in\mathrm{\mathcal{B}}(\mathfrak{M})}\varphi(I)^{k}x^{I},

\displaystyle\mathrm{Sym}_{k}(\varphi;x)

\displaystyle:=\sum_{I\in\mathrm{\mathcal{B}}(\mathfrak{M})}\varphi(I)^{k}x^{I^{c}}.\qed

Note that this definition is compatible with Section
2
, i.e.,

\mathrm{Kir}_{k}(\varphi_{u};x)=\mathrm{Kir}_{k}(u;x)\text{\quad and\quad}\mathrm{Sym}_{k}(\varphi_{u};x)=\mathrm{Sym}_{k}(u;x).

Remark 4.8
.

In order to take into account complex matroids (i.e., matroids over the phase hyperfield, cf.
[
7
,
10
]
), one may want to consider an involution
\varphi\mapsto\overline{\varphi}
. In this case,
\varphi
must be replaced by
\overline{\varphi}
in the definition of the Symanzik polynomials.
∎

Remark 4.9
.

One has to be careful with polynomials with coefficients in a hyperfield. Several definitions are possible (cf.
[
13
]
*Appendix A). However, in this article, every polynomials over hyperfields will be of the form

P(x)=\sum_{I\subseteq E}p_{I}x^{I}

with coefficients
p_{I}
in
\mathbb{F}
(and not a subset of
\mathbb{F}
nor a sum of elements in
\mathbb{F}
). In particular, if
P(x)=\sum_{I}p_{I}x^{I}
and
Q(x)=\sum_{I}q_{I}x^{I}
, we will only add
P
and
Q
if, for any
I
,
p_{I}=0
or
q_{I}=0
. In the same way, we will only multiply
P
and
Q
if for any
I
and
J
such that
p_{I}\neq 0
and
q_{J}\neq 0
, their intersection
I\cap J
is empty.
∎

We now explain how to extend deletion, contraction and duality. Let
\varphi
be a Grassman-Plücker function of rank
r
over
\mathbb{F}
and let
\mathfrak{M}
be its underlying matroid. For any subset
I\subseteq E
, we choose a basis
B_{\varphi/\!I}\in\mathrm{\mathcal{B}}(\mathfrak{M}|I)
, and a basis
B_{\varphi\backslash I}\in\mathrm{\mathcal{B}}(\mathfrak{M}/\!I^{c})
.

Following
[
10
]
, we define
\varphi\backslash I:(E\setminus I)^{\mathrm{rk}(\mathfrak{M}\backslash I)}\to\mathbb{F}
by

(\varphi\backslash I)(J)=\varphi(J\star B_{\varphi\backslash I}).

We also define the restriction:
\varphi|I:=\varphi\backslash I^{c}
.
Similarly, we define
\varphi/\!I:(E\setminus I)^{\mathrm{rk}(\mathfrak{M}/\!I)}\to\mathbb{F}
by

(\varphi/\!I)(J)=\varphi(J\star B_{\varphi/\!I}).

If
I^{\prime}\subseteq E
is disjoint from
I
, we set

(\varphi/\!I^{\prime}\backslash I)(J)=\varphi(J\star B_{\varphi/\!I^{\prime}}\star B_{\varphi\backslash I}).

We define
\widehat{\varphi}:E^{n-\mathrm{rk}(\mathfrak{M})}\to\mathbb{F}
to be the unique Grassman-Plücker function such that, for any subset
J
of
E
of size
n-\mathrm{rk}(\mathfrak{M})
,

\widehat{\varphi}(J)=\mathrm{\sigma}(J\star J^{c})\varphi(J^{c}),

where
\mathrm{\sigma}(J\star J^{c})
denotes the signature of the permutation
J\star J^{c}
. Moreover, we choose

B_{\widehat{\varphi}\backslash I}:=I\setminus B_{\varphi/\!I}\quad\text{and}\quad B_{\widehat{\varphi}/\!I}:=I\setminus B_{\varphi\backslash I}.

As stated in
[
10
]
, the four new functions are Grassman-Plücker functions, and the underlying matroids are respectively
\mathfrak{M}\backslash I
,
\mathfrak{M}/\!I
,
\mathfrak{M}/\!I^{\prime}\backslash I
and
\widehat{\mathfrak{M}}
. Moreover, the functions are independent of the chosen bases up to a nonzero factor (up to a sign for
\widehat{\varphi}
). We also keep the usual rules
\widehat{\widehat{\varphi\phantom{\,}}}=(-1)^{r(n-r)}\varphi
and
\widehat{\varphi/\!I}=\pm\widehat{\varphi}\backslash I
. Article
[
10
]
refers to another article,
[
7
]
, for the proofs. The latter proves the results in the special case of complex matroids. For the sake of completeness, we provide a proof in full generality.

Proof.

Many points are easy to check. We will only check the Grassman-Plücker relations, the independency up to a factor, and the formula
\widehat{\varphi/\!I}=\pm\widehat{\varphi}\backslash I
.

First, we prove the Grassman-Plücker relations for the contraction or the deletion of one element. For some cases, for example the case of the contraction of a loop, there is nothing to prove. Otherwise, for any
e\in E
,
I=(i_{1},\dots,i_{r-1})\in E^{r-1},J=(j_{1},\dots,j_{r-1})\in E^{r-1}
and
\ell\in[r-1]
,

\mathop{\raisebox{-5.0pt}{\scalebox{2}{$\boxplus$}}}\limits_{m=1}^{r-1}\varphi(i_{1},\dots,i_{m-1},j_{\ell},i_{m+1},\dots,i_{r-1},e)\varphi(j_{1},\dots,j_{\ell-1},i_{m},j_{\ell+1},\dots,j_{r-1},e)\\
\boxplus\varphi(i_{1},\dots,i_{r-1},j_{\ell})\varphi(j_{1},\dots,j_{\ell-1},e,j_{\ell+1},\dots,j_{r-1},e)\boxplus-\varphi(I\star(e))\varphi(J\star(e))\ni 0.

Since

\varphi(j_{1},\dots,j_{\ell-1},e,j_{\ell+1},\dots,j_{r-1},e)=0,

we get the Grassman-Plücker relation for
\varphi\backslash\{e\}
and for
\varphi/\!\{e\}
. The Grassman-Plücker relations for a general
\varphi\backslash I
follow easily by induction using that

\displaystyle\varphi\backslash I

\displaystyle=\varphi\backslash B_{\varphi\backslash I}

\displaystyle=(\dots((\varphi\backslash\{b_{s}\})\backslash\{b_{s-1}\})\dots)\backslash\{b_{1}\},

where
B_{\varphi\backslash I}=(b_{1},\dots,b_{s})
. A similar argument works for
\varphi/\!I
and
\varphi/\!I^{\prime}\backslash I
.

For the independency, let us first do a simple case. Let
e,e^{\prime}\in E
be two parallel elements and let
J,J^{\prime}\subseteq E
of size
r-1
. Applying the Grassman-Plücker function to
J\star(e)
and
J^{\prime}\star(e^{\prime})
for
\ell=r
, most of the terms will be zero because

\varphi(\dots,e^{\prime},\dots,e)=0.

Then, we will get

(19)

\varphi(J\star(e^{\prime}))\varphi(J^{\prime}\star(e))=\varphi(J\star(e))\varphi(J^{\prime}\star(e^{\prime})).

Let
I\subseteq E
. Let
J,J^{\prime}\subseteq I
of size
\mathrm{rk}(I)
and
K,K^{\prime}\subseteq I^{c}
of size
r-\mathrm{rk}(I)
. Let us prove that

(20)

\varphi(J\star K)\varphi(J^{\prime}\star K^{\prime})=\varphi(J^{\prime}\star K)\varphi(J\star K^{\prime}).

First, by (
18
),
\varphi(J\star K)
is nonzero if and only if
J\in\mathrm{\mathcal{B}}(\mathfrak{M}|I)
and
K\in\mathrm{\mathcal{B}}(\mathfrak{M}/\!I)
. Thus, either
J,J^{\prime}\in\mathrm{\mathcal{B}}(\mathfrak{M}|I)
and
K,K^{\prime}\in\mathrm{\mathcal{B}}(\mathfrak{M}/\!I)
, and then both members of (
20
) are nonzero, or both members are zero, and then (
20
) is verified. In the first case, assume that
J\neq J^{\prime}
. By the basis exchange property, one can find
e\in J
and
e^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}
such that
e\neq e^{\prime}
and
J_{1}:=J-e+e^{\prime}
is a basis of
\mathfrak{M}|I
. In
\mathfrak{M}|I/\!(J-e)=\mathfrak{M}/\!(J-e)|I
, elements
e
and
e^{\prime}
are parallel. Thus, they are parallel in
\mathfrak{M}/\!(J-e)
. Hence, using Formula (
19
) for
\varphi/\!(J-e)
, we get

(\varphi/\!(J\!-\!e))(K\star(e))\;(\varphi/\!(J\!-\!e))(K^{\prime}\star(e^{\prime}))=(\varphi/\!(J\!-\!e))(K^{\prime}\star(e))\;(\varphi/\!(J\!-\!e))(K\star(e^{\prime})).

Being careful with signs, we deduce

\displaystyle\varphi(K\star J)\varphi(K^{\prime}\star J_{1})=\varphi(K^{\prime}\star J)\varphi(K\star J_{1}),\text{ thus}

\displaystyle\frac{\varphi(K\star J)}{\varphi(K^{\prime}\star J)}=\frac{\varphi(K\star J_{1})}{\varphi(K^{\prime}\star J_{1})}.

Using
|J^{\prime}\setminus J|
times the basis exchange property, we get

\frac{\varphi(K\star J)}{\varphi(K^{\prime}\star J)}=\frac{\varphi(K\star J^{\prime})}{\varphi(K^{\prime}\star J^{\prime})},

which completes the proof of (
20
). As a direct consequence, we get the independency up to a factor of
\varphi/\!I
and
\varphi|I
.

It remains to prove that, if
J\subseteq I^{c}
is of size
\mathrm{rk}(\widehat{\varphi}\backslash I)
, then

(\widehat{\varphi/\!I})(J)=\pm(\widehat{\varphi}\backslash I)(J).

Then, recalling that we chose
B_{\widehat{\varphi}\backslash I}=I\setminus B_{\varphi/\!I}
, we get

\displaystyle(\widehat{\varphi}\backslash I)(J)

\displaystyle=\widehat{\varphi}(J\star B_{\widehat{\varphi}\backslash I})

\displaystyle=\pm\varphi((I^{c}\setminus J)\star B_{\varphi/\!I})

\displaystyle=\pm(\varphi/\!I)(I^{c}\setminus J)

\displaystyle=\pm(\widehat{\varphi/\!I})(J).\qed

We now explain how to generalize Symanzik polynomials with parameters. Let
\varphi^{\prime}
be a Grassman-Plücker function on some set
E\cup W
with
W
a finite set disjoint from
E
. Assume that
W
is independent in
\mathfrak{M}_{\varphi^{\prime}}
. Suppose that
\varphi=\varphi^{\prime}\backslash W
and notice that there is only one way to define
\varphi^{\prime}\backslash W
. We call
\varphi^{\prime}\!/\!\,W
the
quotient of
\varphi
by W
. It is uniquely defined. Then we define

\mathrm{Sym}_{k}(\varphi;W;x):=\mathrm{Sym}_{k}(\varphi^{\prime}\!/\!\,W;x).

Once more, this extends Definition
2.17
.

4.4.
Deletion-contraction formula

Thanks to the definitions of last subsections, we can state the generalization of the deletion-contraction formula (cf. Equation (
4
) in the introduction). From those definitions, the following result is immediate.

Proposition 4.10

(Deletion-contraction formula)
.

Let
e\in E
. We set

N_{1}(e):=\begin{cases}0&\text{if $e$ is a loop in $\mathfrak{M}$,}\\
x_{e}&\text{otherwise,}\end{cases}\quad\text{and}\quad N_{2}(e):=\begin{cases}0&\text{if $e$ is free in $\mathfrak{M}$,}\\
1&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Then,

\mathrm{Kir}_{k}(\varphi;x)=N_{1}(e)\mathrm{Kir}_{k}(\varphi/\!\{e\};x_{E-e})+N_{2}(e)\mathrm{Kir}_{k}(\varphi\backslash\{e\};x_{E-e}).

Moreover, setting

\widehat{N}_{1}(e):=\begin{cases}0&\text{if $e$ is a loop in $\mathfrak{M}$,}\\
1&\text{otherwise,}\end{cases}\quad\text{and}\quad\widehat{N}_{2}(e):=\begin{cases}0&\text{if $e$ is free in $\mathfrak{M}$,}\\
x_{e}&\text{otherwise,}\end{cases}

we have

\mathrm{Sym}_{k}(\varphi;x)=\widehat{N}_{1}(e)\mathrm{Sym}_{k}(\varphi/\!\{e\};x_{E-e})+\widehat{N}_{2}(e)\mathrm{Sym}_{k}(\varphi\backslash\{e\};x_{E-e}).

The notations of the previous proposition follows those of
[
25
]
. This article describes Universal Tutte characters which unify objects behaving like Tutte polynomials. By the following proposition, we show that the polynomials
\mathrm{Kir}_{k}
and
\mathrm{Sym}_{k}
belong to this big family. In particular, we have the following formulæ.

Proposition 4.11
.

Let
I\subseteq E
. We extend
N_{1}
and
N_{2}
by

N_{1}(\varphi|I)=\begin{cases}0&\text{if $\mathfrak{M}|I$ is not the free matroid,}\\
\prod_{i\in I}x_{i}&\text{otherwise,}\end{cases}\qquad N_{2}(\varphi/\!I)=\begin{cases}0&\text{if $\mathrm{rk}(I)>0$,}\\
1&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Then,

\mathrm{Kir}_{k}(\varphi;x)=\sum_{I\subseteq E}N_{1}(\varphi|I)\tau(\varphi|I/\!I)N_{2}(\varphi/\!I),

where, denoting by
\varnothing
the
0
-tuple,

\tau(\varphi|I/\!I):=(\varphi|I/\!I)(\varnothing)^{k}.

Moreover,

\widehat{N}_{1}(\varphi|I)=\begin{cases}0&\text{if $\mathfrak{M}|I$ is not the free matroid,}\\
1&\text{otherwise,}\end{cases}\qquad\widehat{N}_{2}(\varphi/\!I)=\begin{cases}0&\text{if $\mathrm{rk}(I)>0$,}\\
\prod_{i\in I^{c}}x_{i}&\text{otherwise,}\end{cases}

we have

\mathrm{Sym}_{k}(\varphi;x)=\sum_{I\subseteq E}\widehat{N}_{1}(\varphi|I)\tau(\varphi|I/\!I)\widehat{N}_{2}(\varphi/\!I).

One might have noticed that
\widehat{N}_{2}
in Proposition
4.11
is not an extension of
\widehat{N}_{2}
in Proposition
4.10
. Actually,
\widehat{N}_{2}(e)
must be understood as
\widehat{N}_{2}(\varphi/\!\{e\}^{c})
(cf.
[
25
]
).

To understand why this last proposition holds, notice that a term of the right-hand side sum is zero if
I
is not a basis of
\mathfrak{M}
. If
I
is a basis, then, for the Kirchhoff polynomial for instance, we have

N_{1}(\varphi|I)\tau(\varphi|I/\!I)N_{2}(\varphi/\!I)=\prod_{i\in I}x_{i}\cdot(\varphi|I/\!I)(\varnothing)^{k}.

Since
I
is a basis,
B_{\varphi|I}=\varnothing
and
B_{\varphi/\!I}=I
. Thus,

\varphi|I/\!I(\varnothing)=\varphi(I).

Remark 4.12
.

Following this idea, one might define the Tutte polynomial of
\varphi
by

\sum_{I\subseteq E}(x-1)^{\mathrm{rk}(E)-\mathrm{rk}(I)}(y-1)^{|I|-\mathrm{rk}(I)}\varphi|I/\!I(\varnothing)^{k}.

Unfortunately, this formula strongly depends on the bases of
B_{\varphi|I}
and
B_{\varphi/\!I}
(unless for terms where
I
is a basis).
∎

4.5.
Partial factorization

Let us conclude this section by a generalization of the useful formula of partial factorization (cf. Equation (
5
) in the introduction).

Let

P(x)=\sum_{J}p_{J}x^{J}

be a multivariate polynomial. Let
I
be a subset of
[n]
. We define the set of the degrees of the monomials of
P
with respect to the variables
x_{I}
as

\displaystyle\mathrm{degs}_{x_{I}}(P(x)):=\{|J\cap I|\,\big|\,p_{J}\neq 0\}.

Here is the generalization of the formula.

Proposition 4.13
.

Let
I\subseteq E
. Then

\mathrm{Kir}_{k}(\varphi;x)=\frac{1}{\varphi|I/\!I(\varnothing)^{k}}\mathrm{Kir}_{k}(\varphi|I;x_{I})\mathrm{Kir}_{k}(\varphi/\!I;x_{I^{c}})+R_{\varphi,I}(x)

for some polynomial
R_{\varphi,I}(x)
verifying

\displaystyle\max\mathrm{degs}_{x_{I}}(R_{\varphi,I}(x))<\deg(\mathrm{Kir}_{k}(\varphi|I;x_{I}))=\mathrm{rk}(I),

\displaystyle\min\mathrm{degs}_{x_{I^{c}}}(R_{\varphi,I}(x))>\deg(\mathrm{Kir}_{k}(\varphi/\!I;x_{I^{c}}))=\mathrm{rk}(\mathfrak{M}/\!I).

Similarly,

\mathrm{Sym}_{k}(\varphi;x)=\frac{1}{\varphi|I/\!I(\varnothing)^{k}}\mathrm{Sym}_{k}(\varphi|I;x_{I})\mathrm{Sym}_{k}(\varphi/\!I;x_{I^{c}})+\widehat{R}_{\varphi,I}(x),

for some polynomial
\widehat{R}_{\varphi,I}(x)
verifying

\displaystyle\min\mathrm{degs}_{x_{I}}(\widehat{R}_{\varphi,I}(x))>\deg(\mathrm{Sym}_{k}(\varphi|I;x_{I})),

\displaystyle\max\mathrm{degs}_{x_{I^{c}}}(\widehat{R}_{\varphi,I}(x))<\deg(\mathrm{Sym}_{k}(\varphi/\!I;x_{I^{c}})).

Proof.

We have

\mathrm{Kir}_{k}(\varphi;x)=\sum_{B\in\mathrm{\mathcal{B}}(\mathfrak{M})}\varphi(B)^{k}x_{I}^{B\cap I}x_{I^{c}}^{B\cap I^{c}}.

If
B\in\mathrm{\mathcal{B}}(\mathfrak{M})
, then, by (
16
),
|B\cap I^{c}|
is minimal if and only if
|B\cap I|
is maximal if and only if
B\cap I
is a basis of
\mathfrak{M}|I
. By (
17
), we can cut the sum in two parts in the following way:

\mathrm{Kir}_{k}(\varphi;x)=\sum_{B^{\prime}\in\mathrm{\mathcal{B}}(\mathfrak{M}|I)}\sum_{B^{\prime\prime}\in\mathrm{\mathcal{B}}(\mathfrak{M}/\!I)}\varphi(B^{\prime}\star B^{\prime\prime})^{k}x_{I}^{B^{\prime}}x_{I^{c}}^{B^{\prime\prime}}+R_{\varphi,I}(x),

where

R_{\varphi,I}(x):=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}B\in\mathrm{\mathcal{B}}(\mathfrak{M})\\
|B\cap I|<\mathrm{rk}(I)\end{subarray}}\varphi(B)^{k}x^{B}.

The sum is of degree
\mathrm{rk}(I)
in
x_{I}
and
\mathrm{rk}(\mathfrak{M}/\!I)
in
x_{I^{c}}
. Moreover, the degree of each term of
R_{\varphi,I}(x)
is less than
\mathrm{rk}(I)
in
x_{I}
and greater than
\mathrm{rk}(\mathfrak{M}/\!I)
in
x_{I^{c}}
.

We also have

\mathrm{Kir}_{k}(\varphi|I;x_{I})\mathrm{Kir}_{k}(\varphi/\!I;x_{I^{c}})=\sum_{B^{\prime}\in\mathrm{\mathcal{B}}(\mathfrak{M}|I)}(\varphi|I)(B^{\prime})^{k}x_{I}^{B^{\prime}}\sum_{B^{\prime\prime}\in\mathrm{\mathcal{B}}(\mathfrak{M}/\!I)}(\varphi/\!I)(B^{\prime\prime})^{k}x_{I^{c}}^{B^{\prime\prime}}.

Hence, it remains to prove that if
B^{\prime}\in\mathrm{\mathcal{B}}(\mathfrak{M}|I)
and if
B^{\prime\prime}\in\mathrm{\mathcal{B}}(\mathfrak{M}/\!I)
, then

(\varphi|I)(B^{\prime})\,(\varphi/\!I)(B^{\prime\prime})=\pm(\varphi|I/\!I)(\varnothing)\,\varphi(B^{\prime}\star B^{\prime\prime}).

This is equivalent to

\varphi(B^{\prime}\star B_{\varphi|I})\varphi(B^{\prime\prime}\star B_{\varphi/\!I})=\pm\varphi(B_{\varphi/\!I}\star B_{\varphi|I})\varphi(B^{\prime}\star B^{\prime\prime}).

But this is just a consequence of (
20
). The proof for Symanzik polynomials is similar.
∎

5.
Exchange graph for matroids

This section could seem out of context: we will not talk about Symanzik polynomials. However, we need Corollary
5.14
below in the next section. Theorem
5.13
and its corollaries are interesting combinatorial results about connected components of what we call the exchange graph of a matroid. These results generalize Theorem 2.13 of
[
4
]
to the matroids, and they go further in the study of the exchange graph.

Notations and reminders about matroids can be found in Section
4.1
. In this section, we fix a matroid
\mathfrak{M}=(E,\mathfrak{I})
. We set
r:=\mathrm{rk}(\mathfrak{M})
. If
I\subseteq E
,
\mathrm{fr}(I)
will denote the set of elements independent from
I
, i.e., the complement of
\mathrm{cl}(I)
in
E
.

We are interested in finding the different connected components of some interesting subgraphs of the exchange graph of a matroid we define right below.

Definition 5.1
.

The
exchange graph

\mathcal{G}=(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E})
associated to
\mathfrak{M}
is the graph with vertex set
\mathcal{V}:=\mathfrak{I}\times\mathfrak{I}
and edge set
\mathcal{E}
such that two vertices
(I_{1},I_{2})
and
(I^{\prime}_{1},I^{\prime}_{2})
are adjacent if there exists
i\in E
such that either,
I^{\prime}_{1}=I_{1}+i
and
I^{\prime}_{2}=I_{2}-i
, or,
I^{\prime}_{1}=I_{1}-i
and
I^{\prime}_{2}=I_{2}+i
.
∎

We fix
\mathcal{G}=(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E})
the exchange graph of
\mathfrak{M}
. If
\mathcal{U}
is a subset of
\mathcal{V}
, then
\mathcal{G}[\mathcal{U}]
denotes the induced subgraph of
\mathcal{G}
with vertex set
\mathcal{U}
, i.e., the subgraph of
\mathcal{G}
of vertex set
\mathcal{U}
and edge set all the edges connecting pairs of vertices in
\mathcal{U}
.
If
p,q\in[0\,\raisebox{0.86108pt}{.}\,\raisebox{0.86108pt}{.}\,r]
, then we set
\mathcal{V}_{p,q}:=\mathfrak{I}_{p}\times\mathfrak{I}_{q}
.
Moreover, if
p\not=0
and
q\not=r
, we define the bipartite graph
\mathcal{G}_{p,q}:=\mathcal{G}[\mathcal{V}_{p,q}\sqcup\mathcal{V}_{p-1,q+1}]
.

Remark 5.2
.

If
p\in[1\,\raisebox{0.86108pt}{.}\,\raisebox{0.86108pt}{.}\,r]
and
q\in[0\,\raisebox{0.86108pt}{.}\,\raisebox{0.86108pt}{.}\,r-1]
, then we have a natural graph isomorphism:

\begin{array}[]{rrl}\Phi_{p,q}:&\mathcal{G}_{p,q}\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }&\mathcal{G}_{q+1,p-1},\\
&(I_{1},I_{2})\in\mathcal{V}_{p,q}\sqcup\mathcal{V}_{p-1,q+1}\xmapsto{\phantom{\ \sim\ }}&(I_{2},I_{1})\in\mathcal{V}_{q,p}\sqcup\mathcal{V}_{q+1,p-1}.\end{array}\qed

There are two important invariants in connected components of
\mathcal{G}
. They correspond to Definitions
5.3
and
5.6
below.

Definition 5.3
.

If
I,J
are two non necessarily disjoint sets, we write
I\uplus J
for the multiset containing elements of
I
and, disjointly, elements of
J
(such that elements in
I\cap J
appear in
I\uplus J
with multiplicity
2
).
∎

Abusing notation, if
(U,V)
and
(I,J)
are two ordered pairs of sets, then we write
(U,V)\subseteq(I,J)
if
U\subseteq I
and
V\subseteq J
. That defines a partial order on ordered pairs of sets.

Definition 5.4
.

If
(I,J)\in\mathfrak{I}\times\mathfrak{I}
is an ordered pair of independent sets and if
(U,V)
is another one, we say that
(U,V)
is a
codependent pair of
(I,J)
if
(U,V)\subseteq(I,J)
and
\mathrm{cl}(U)=\mathrm{cl}(V)
.
∎

Claim 5.5
.

Let
(I,J)\in\mathfrak{I}\times\mathfrak{I}
be an ordered pair of independent sets. Let
(U,V),(U^{\prime},V^{\prime})\in\mathfrak{I}\times\mathfrak{I}
be two codependent pairs of
(I,J)
. Then
(U\cup U^{\prime},V\cup V^{\prime})
is a codependent pair of
(I,J)
.

Proof.

Clearly
(U\cup U^{\prime},V\cup V^{\prime})\subseteq(I,J)
. And,

\mathrm{cl}(U\cup U^{\prime})=\mathrm{cl}(\mathrm{cl}(U)\cup\mathrm{cl}(U^{\prime}))=\mathrm{cl}(\mathrm{cl}(V)\cup\mathrm{cl}(V^{\prime}))=\mathrm{cl}(V\cup V^{\prime}).\qed

Thanks to the previous claim, and noticing that
(\emptyset,\emptyset)
is always a codependent pair, one can state the following definition.

Definition 5.6
.

If
(I,J)\in\mathfrak{I}\times\mathfrak{I}
is an ordered pair of independent sets, we call
maximal codependent pair (or MCP)
of
I
and
J
, denoted by
\mathrm{MCP}(I,J)
, the unique maximal codependent pair of
(I,J)
for the inclusion.
∎

Proposition 5.7
.

If
(I,J)\in\mathcal{V}
, then
I\uplus J
and
\mathrm{MCP}(I,J)
are invariant in the connected component of
(I,J)
in
\mathcal{G}
, i.e., if
(I^{\prime},J^{\prime})
is any element of the connected component of
(I,J)
, then

I\uplus J=I^{\prime}\uplus J^{\prime}\text{ and }\mathrm{MCP}(I,J)=\mathrm{MCP}(I^{\prime},J^{\prime}).

Proof.

Let
(I,J)
and
(J^{\prime},I^{\prime})
be two neighbors in
\mathcal{V}
such that there exists
i\in E
such that
I^{\prime}=J+i
, and
I=J^{\prime}+i
. One has

I\uplus J=(J^{\prime}+i)\uplus J=J^{\prime}\uplus(J+i)=J^{\prime}\uplus I^{\prime}.

Moreover, if
(U,V):=\mathrm{MCP}(I,J)
, since
i\in\mathrm{fr}(J)
,
i\not\in\mathrm{cl}(V)
, and so
i\not\in U
. Thus,
\mathrm{MCP}(I,J)=\mathrm{MCP}(I-i,J)=\mathrm{MCP}(J^{\prime},J)
. Using the same argument on
(J^{\prime},I^{\prime})
, one obtains the equality
\mathrm{MCP}(I,J)=\mathrm{MCP}(J^{\prime},I^{\prime})
. The end of the proof is now straightforward.
∎

Let us fix
p\in[1\,\raisebox{0.86108pt}{.}\,\raisebox{0.86108pt}{.}\,r]
and
q\in[0\,\raisebox{0.86108pt}{.}\,\raisebox{0.86108pt}{.}\,r-1]
. The nice result is that the two invariants of Proposition
5.7
form a complete set of invariants (see Corollary
5.15
). This is also true for
\mathcal{G}_{p,q}
if we ignore isolated vertices. That is why we first study which vertices are isolated.

Proposition 5.8
.

Let
(I,J)
be a vertex of
\mathcal{G}
. Then:

(1)

(I,J)
is an isolated vertex of
\mathcal{G}
if and only if
\mathrm{cl}(I)=\mathrm{cl}(J)
, i.e.,
\mathrm{MCP}(I,J)=(I,J)
;

(2)

if
(I,J)\in\mathcal{G}_{p,q}
, then
(I,J)
is an isolated vertex of
\mathcal{G}_{p,q}
if and only if one of the two following cases happens.

•

either
(I,J)\in\mathcal{V}_{p,q}
and
I\subseteq\mathrm{cl}(J)
,

•

or
(I,J)\in\mathcal{V}_{p-1,q+1}
and
J\subseteq\mathrm{cl}(I)
;

(3)

if
p=q+1
,
\mathcal{G}_{p,q}
has no isolated vertex.

Proof.

Let
(I,J)\in\mathcal{V}_{p,q}
. The neighborhood of
(I,J)
in
\mathcal{G}_{p,q}
consists of all vertices of the form
(I-i,J+i)
for
i\in I\cap\mathrm{fr}(J)
. Thus, Point (
2
) is clear. Then,
(I,J)
is isolated in
\mathcal{G}
if and only if it is isolated in
\mathcal{G}_{p,q}
and in
\mathcal{G}_{p+1,q-1}
(if
p\neq r
and
q\neq 0
). By Point (
2
), this is equivalent to
I\subseteq\mathrm{cl}(J)
and
J\subseteq\mathrm{cl}(I)
, i.e., to
\mathrm{cl}(I)=\mathrm{cl}(J)
(notice that the second inclusion is trivial if
p=r
or
q=0
). Finally, if
p>q
,
\mathrm{rk}(I)>\mathrm{rk}(J)
, thus
I
cannot be a subset of
\mathrm{cl}(J)
. Thus, if
p=q+1
, we can get Point (
3
) from Point (
2
).
∎

Let us introduce another definition below. Actually, it is equivalent to Definition
5.6
.

Definition 5.9
.

Let
(I,J)\in\mathcal{V}_{p,q}
and let
\mathcal{W}
be the connected component of
(I,J)
in
\mathcal{G}_{p,q}
. Then we define the
ordered pair of fixed elements of
(I,J)
by

\mathrm{PFE}(I,J):=\bigg(\bigcap_{(U,V)\in\mathcal{W}}U,\ \bigcap_{(U,V)\in\mathcal{W}}V\bigg).\qed

Proposition 5.10
.

If
(I,J)
is a non-isolated vertex of
\mathcal{G}_{p,q}
, then

\mathrm{MCP}(I,J)=\mathrm{PFE}(I,J).

We need the following lemma which is a direct consequence of Proposition 1.1.6 of
[
46
]
.

Lemma 5.11
.

If
U\in\mathfrak{I}
and
i\in\mathrm{cl}(U)
, then
\{C\subseteq U\,\big|\,i\in\mathrm{cl}(C)\}
admits a least element for the inclusion.

Proof of the proposition.

Let
(P_{1},P_{2}):=\mathrm{PFE}(I,J)
. Denote by
\mathcal{W}
the set of vertices in the connected component of
(I,J)
in
\mathcal{G}_{p,q}
and by
\mathcal{W}_{p,q}
, resp. by
\mathcal{W}_{p-1,q+1}
, the intersection
\mathcal{W}\cap\mathcal{V}_{p,q}
, resp.
\mathcal{W}\cap\mathcal{V}_{p-1,q+1}
. By Proposition
5.7
, it is clear that
\mathrm{MCP}(I,J)\subseteq\mathrm{PFE}(I,J)
.

In order to prove the other inclusion, we introduce the two following sets

Q_{1}:=\bigcap_{(J_{1},I_{1})\in\mathcal{W}_{p-1,q+1}}\mathrm{cl}(J_{1}),\qquad Q_{2}:=\bigcap_{(I_{2},J_{2})\in\mathcal{W}_{p,q}}\mathrm{cl}(J_{2}).

Clearly,

(21)

P_{1}\subseteq Q_{1}\text{ and }P_{2}\subseteq Q_{2}.

The interesting property of these sets is that

(22)

Q_{1}=Q_{2}.

In order to prove this, it is enough to show that any element of
E
which is not in
Q_{2}
, is not in
Q_{1}
either. Let
i
be an element of
E
which is not in
Q_{2}
. There exists
(I_{2},J_{2})\in\mathcal{W}_{p,q}
such that
i\not\in\mathrm{cl}(J_{2})
. If
i\not\in\mathrm{cl}(I_{2})
, then
i\not\in\mathrm{cl}(J_{1})
for any neighbor
(J_{1},I_{1})
of
(I_{2},J_{2})
, and so
i\not\in Q_{1}
. Otherwise, assume
i\in\mathrm{cl}(I_{2})
. By Lemma
5.11
, there exists a least element for the inclusion
C\subseteq I_{2}
such that
i\in\mathrm{cl}(C)
. Since
i\not\in\mathrm{cl}(J_{2})
, we have
C\not\subseteq\mathrm{cl}(J_{2})
. Let
j\in C\cap\mathrm{fr}(J_{2})
. One obtains that
(I_{2}-j,J_{2}+j)\in\mathcal{W}_{p-1,q+1}
, and that
i\not\in\mathrm{cl}(I_{2}-j)
since
C\not\subseteq I_{2}-j
. Thus,
i\not\in Q_{1}
.

The third result is

(23)

Q_{2}\subseteq\mathrm{cl}(P_{2}).

To see this, let
i\in Q_{2}
. Let
(J_{1},I_{1})
be a neighbor of
(I,J)
. One has
i\in\mathrm{cl}(J)
, and so
i\in\mathrm{cl}(I_{1})
(since
J\subseteq I_{1}
). Thus, by Lemma
5.11
, one can choose
C
, resp.
C_{1}
, minimal for the inclusion in
J
, resp.
I_{1}
, such that
i
is in the closure of
C
, resp.
C_{1}
. By minimality,
C=C_{1}
. Hence, by connectivity, for all
(U,V)\in\mathcal{W}
,
C\subseteq V
. Thus, we have
C\subseteq P_{2}
, and so
i\in\mathrm{cl}(P_{2})
.

We now have all the needed intermediate results. Equations (
21
), (
22
) and (
23
) imply that
P_{1}\subseteq Q_{1}=Q_{2}\subseteq\mathrm{cl}(P_{2})
, and so
\mathrm{cl}(P_{1})\subseteq\mathrm{cl}(P_{2})
. Using a symmetric argument, we obtain that
\mathrm{cl}(P_{2})\subseteq\mathrm{cl}(P_{1})
and so
\mathrm{cl}(P_{1})=\mathrm{cl}(P_{2})
. Thus,
(P_{1},P_{2})
is a dependent ordered pair of
(I,J)
. In particular,
(P_{1},P_{2})=\mathrm{PFE}(I,J)\subseteq\mathrm{MCP}(I,J)
.
∎

In what follow, we will use the following lemma, which is easy to prove.

Lemma 5.12
.

Let
U,U^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{I}
be such that
|U|=|U^{\prime}|
and
\mathrm{fr}(U)\not=\mathrm{fr}(U^{\prime})
. Then,
U\cap\mathrm{fr}(U^{\prime})
and
U^{\prime}\cap\mathrm{fr}(U)
are nonempty.

We can now state the main theorem of this section.

Theorem 5.13
.

Let
(I,J)
and
(I^{\prime},J^{\prime})
be two non-isolated vertices of
\mathcal{G}_{p,q}
. Then
(I,J)
and
(I^{\prime},J^{\prime})
are in the same connected component of
\mathcal{G}_{p,q}
if and only if

I\uplus J=I^{\prime}\uplus J^{\prime}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathrm{MCP}(I,J)=\mathrm{MCP}(I^{\prime},J^{\prime}).

Proof.

The forward direction is given by Proposition
5.7
.

Let
(I_{0},J_{0})
and
(I_{0}^{\prime},J_{0}^{\prime})
be two non-isolated vertices of
\mathcal{G}_{p,q}
such that
I_{0}\uplus J_{0}=I_{0}^{\prime}\uplus J_{0}^{\prime}
and
\mathrm{MCP}(I_{0},J_{0})=\mathrm{MCP}(I^{\prime}_{0},J^{\prime}_{0})
. Denote by
\mathcal{W}
, resp.
\mathcal{W}^{\prime}
, the set of vertices in the connected component of
(I_{0},J_{0})
, resp. of
(I_{0}^{\prime},J_{0}^{\prime})
, in
\mathcal{G}_{p,q}
. Define
\mathcal{W}_{p,q}:=\mathcal{W}\cap\mathcal{V}_{p,q}
, and define similarly
\mathcal{W}_{p-1,q+1},\mathcal{W}^{\prime}_{p,q},\mathcal{W}^{\prime}_{p-1,q+1}
. Since both vertices are assumed to be non-isolated, the four previous sets are nonempty. If
(I,J)
and
(I^{\prime},J^{\prime})
are two elements of
\mathcal{V}_{p,q}
, we set

d((I,J),(I^{\prime},J^{\prime})):=|I\setminus I^{\prime}|=p-|I\cap I^{\prime}|.

Now, set
(I,J)\in\mathcal{W}_{p,q}
and
(I^{\prime},J^{\prime})\in\mathcal{W}^{\prime}_{p,q}
such that
d((I,J),(I^{\prime},J^{\prime}))
is minimal. Set
d:=d((I,J),(I^{\prime},J^{\prime}))
. We must show that
d=0
.

For the sake of a contradiction, assume that
d\not=0
. Set
(P_{1},P_{2}):=\mathrm{PFE}(I,J)
and
(P^{\prime}_{1},P^{\prime}_{2}):=\mathrm{PFE}(I^{\prime},J^{\prime})
. Assume for now the following equation.

(24)

\forall(\widetilde{I},\widetilde{J})\in\mathcal{W}_{p,q},J\setminus J^{\prime}\subseteq\widetilde{J}.

First, if
(\widetilde{J},\widetilde{I})\in\mathcal{W}_{p-1,q+1}
, one can find a neighbor
(\widetilde{I}_{1},\widetilde{J}_{1})\in\mathcal{W}_{p,q}
of
(\widetilde{J},\widetilde{I})
. We have
J\setminus J^{\prime}\subseteq\widetilde{J}_{1}\subseteq\widetilde{I}
. Thus, one can replace
\mathcal{W}_{p,q}
by
\mathcal{W}
in (
24
), i.e.,

J\setminus J^{\prime}\subseteq P_{2}.

Secondly, since
d\neq 0
,
J\neq J^{\prime}
. Hence

J\setminus J^{\prime}\neq\emptyset.

Thirdly,
P^{\prime}_{2}\subseteq J^{\prime}
. Thus

(J\setminus J^{\prime})\cap P^{\prime}_{2}=\emptyset.

Finally, the three last equations implies that
P_{2}\neq P_{2}^{\prime}
. Therefore
\mathrm{PFE}(I,J)\neq\mathrm{PFE}(I^{\prime},J^{\prime})
. By Proposition
5.10
,
\mathrm{MCP}(I,J)\neq\mathrm{MCP}(I^{\prime},J^{\prime})
. Applying Proposition
5.7
,
\mathrm{MCP}(I_{0},J_{0})\neq\mathrm{MCP}(I_{0}^{\prime},J_{0}^{\prime})
. This contradicts our assumption.

It remains to prove Equation (
24
). We note first that

(25)

I\cap J=I^{\prime}\cap J^{\prime}.

This can be easily deduced from
I\uplus J=I^{\prime}\uplus J^{\prime}
.

Next, we have

(26)

\mathrm{fr}(J)=\mathrm{fr}(J^{\prime}).

To see this, suppose for the sake of a contradiction that this equation is false. By Lemma
5.12
, there exists an
i\in J\,\cap\,\mathrm{fr}(J^{\prime})
. One can see that
i\in I^{\prime}
, because
i\in(I\cup J)\setminus J^{\prime}
. Moreover, let
j\in\mathrm{fr}(I^{\prime}-i)\,\cap\,I
, which exists because
\mathrm{rk}(I^{\prime}-i)<\mathrm{rk}(I)
. Similarly,
j\in J^{\prime}+i
. One obtains that
(I^{\prime}-i+j,J^{\prime}+i-j)\in\mathcal{W}_{p,q}^{\prime}
and
(I^{\prime}-i+j)\,\cap\,I=(I^{\prime}\,\cap\,I)+j
, because
j\in I
and
i\not\in I
(otherwise one would have
i\in I\,\cap\,J
, and so
i\in I^{\prime}\,\cap\,J^{\prime}
by (
25
); but
i\in\mathrm{fr}(J^{\prime})
). The last equality contradicts minimality of
d
.

Now set
i\in\mathrm{fr}(J)\,\cap\,I
, which exists by Proposition
5.8
, (
2
). One has directly by (
26
) that
i\in\mathrm{fr}(J^{\prime})
, and so
i\in I\,\cap\,I^{\prime}
. Then we prove that

(27)

\displaystyle\mathrm{fr}(I-i)=\mathrm{fr}(I^{\prime}-i).

Otherwise, by Lemma
5.12
, one could set
j\in(I^{\prime}-i)\,\cap\,\mathrm{fr}(I-i)
and
j^{\prime}\in(I-i)\,\cap\,\mathrm{fr}(I^{\prime}-i)
. But
(I-i+j,J+i-j)\in\mathcal{W}_{p,q}
and
(I^{\prime}-i+j^{\prime},J^{\prime}+i-j^{\prime})\in\mathcal{W}_{p,q}^{\prime}
. Moreover
(I-i+j)\,\cap\,(I^{\prime}-i+j^{\prime})=(I\,\cap\,I^{\prime})-i+j+j^{\prime}
. Once more, that contradicts minimality of
d
.

If
(J_{1},I_{1})
is a neighbor of
(I,J)
, and if
(I_{2},J_{2})
is a neighbor of
(J_{1},I_{1})
, then there exists an
i\in\mathrm{fr}(J)
such that
I_{1}=J+i
, and there exists a
j\in\mathrm{fr}(J_{1})=\mathrm{fr}(I-i)
such that
I_{2}=I-i+j
. By (
26
),
i\in\mathrm{fr}(J^{\prime})
, and, by (
27
),
j\in\mathrm{fr}(I^{\prime}-i)
. Setting
(I_{2}^{\prime},J_{2}^{\prime}):=(I^{\prime}-i+j,J^{\prime}+i-j)
, one obtains that
(I_{2}^{\prime},J_{2}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{W}_{p,q}^{\prime}
. First observe that

(28)

\displaystyle J_{2}\setminus J_{2}^{\prime}=(J+i-j)\setminus(J^{\prime}+i-j)=J\setminus J^{\prime},

(29)

\displaystyle I_{2}\setminus I_{2}^{\prime}=(I-i+j)\setminus(I^{\prime}-i+j)=I\setminus I^{\prime}.

Hence,
d((I_{2},J_{2}),(I^{\prime}_{2},J^{\prime}_{2}))=d
is minimal. We infer that this two vertices also verifies Equations (
25
), (
26
) and (
27
).

Now let
(\widetilde{I},\widetilde{J})\in\mathcal{W}_{p,q}
. Since
(I_{2},J_{2})
has been arbitrarily chosen among neighbors of neighbors of
(I,J)
, one can in the same way choose a path in
\mathcal{W}
:

((I,J),(J_{1},I_{1}),(I_{2},J_{2}),(J_{3},I_{3}),\dots,(I_{n},J_{n})=(\widetilde{I},\widetilde{J})).

To this path corresponds a path in
\mathcal{W}^{\prime}
:

((I^{\prime},J^{\prime}),(J^{\prime}_{1},I^{\prime}_{1}),(I^{\prime}_{2},J^{\prime}_{2}),(J^{\prime}_{3},I^{\prime}_{3}),\dots,(I^{\prime}_{n},J^{\prime}_{n})).

Set
(\widetilde{I}^{\prime},\widetilde{J}^{\prime}):=(I^{\prime}_{n},J^{\prime}_{n})
. Equations (
25
), (
26
), (
27
), (
28
) and (
29
) propagate along the paths. In particular,
\widetilde{J}\setminus\widetilde{J}^{\prime}=J\setminus J^{\prime}
. Hence,

J\setminus J^{\prime}\subseteq\widetilde{J}.

We conclude that (
24
) is true, which prove the theorem.
∎

This first corollary will be useful in Section
6
.

Corollary 5.14
.

Let
(I,J),(I^{\prime},J^{\prime})
be two arbitrary vertices of
\mathcal{G}_{r,r-1}
. Then
(I,J)
and
(I^{\prime},J^{\prime})
are in the same connected component of
\mathcal{G}_{r,r-1}
if and only if

I\uplus J=I^{\prime}\uplus J^{\prime}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathrm{MCP}(I,J)=\mathrm{MCP}(I^{\prime},J^{\prime}).

Proof.

It suffices to combine Theorem
5.13
with Point (
3
) of Proposition
5.8
.
∎

The second corollary extends Theorem
5.13
to the case of the whole exchange graph.

Corollary 5.15
.

Let
(I,J)
,
(I^{\prime},J^{\prime})
be two arbitrary vertices of
\mathcal{G}
. Then
(I,J)
and
(I^{\prime},J^{\prime})
are in the same connected component of
\mathcal{G}
if and only if

I\uplus J=I^{\prime}\uplus J^{\prime}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathrm{MCP}(I,J)=\mathrm{MCP}(I^{\prime},J^{\prime}).

Proof.

The forward direction is given by Proposition
5.7
. For the other direction, let
(I,J)
and
(I^{\prime},J^{\prime})
be two vertices of
\mathcal{G}
such that
I\uplus J=I^{\prime}\uplus J^{\prime}
and
\mathrm{MCP}(I,J)=\mathrm{MCP}(I^{\prime},J^{\prime})
. Let

\displaystyle p:=\left\lceil\frac{|I|+|J|}{2}\right\rceil=\left\lceil\frac{|I^{\prime}|+|J^{\prime}|}{2}\right\rceil,

\displaystyle q:=\left\lfloor\frac{|I|+|J|}{2}\right\rfloor=\left\lfloor\frac{|I^{\prime}|+|J^{\prime}|}{2}\right\rfloor.

Note that
p-q
is either zero or one. If
|J|>|I|
, then
J\cap\mathrm{fr}(I)
is nonempty. Let
j
be an element of this set. Then
(I+j,J-j)
is adjacent to
(I,J)
. Iterating this process, it is clear that, if
|J|>|I|
, the connected component of
(I,J)
contains a vertex of
\mathcal{G}_{p,q}
. Actually, this is still true if
|J|\leqslant|I|
putting elements of
I
in
J
and stopping at the right time. Let
(\widetilde{I},\widetilde{J})
, resp.
(\widetilde{I}^{\prime},\widetilde{J}^{\prime})
, be an element of
\mathcal{G}_{p,q}
in the connected component of
(I,J)
, resp. of
(I^{\prime},J^{\prime})
. We have
\widetilde{I}\uplus\widetilde{J}=\widetilde{I}^{\prime}\uplus\widetilde{J}^{\prime}
and
\mathrm{MCP}(\widetilde{I},\widetilde{J})=\mathrm{MCP}(\widetilde{I}^{\prime},\widetilde{J}^{\prime})
. Thus,
(\widetilde{I},\widetilde{J})
and
(\widetilde{I}^{\prime},\widetilde{J}^{\prime})
are connected in
\mathcal{G}_{p,q}
, thus in
\mathcal{G}
, provided they are not isolated in
\mathcal{G}_{p,q}
. There are two possibilities.

If
p=q+1
, then they cannot be isolated by Proposition
5.8
(
3
). Otherwise,
p=q
. In this case, suppose, for example, that
(\widetilde{I},\widetilde{J})
is isolated in
\mathcal{G}_{p,q}
. Then, by Proposition
5.8
(
2
),
\widetilde{I}\subseteq\mathrm{cl}(\widetilde{J})
. But, since
|\widetilde{J}|=|\widetilde{I}|
,
\mathrm{cl}(\widetilde{J})=\mathrm{cl}(\widetilde{I})
, thus
\mathrm{MCP}(\widetilde{I},\widetilde{J})=(\widetilde{I},\widetilde{J})
, and so
\mathrm{MCP}(\widetilde{I}^{\prime},\widetilde{J}^{\prime})=(\widetilde{I},\widetilde{J})
. Looking at cardinalities, this last equality implies that
(\widetilde{I},\widetilde{J})=(\widetilde{I}^{\prime},\widetilde{J}^{\prime})
.

In every case,
(\widetilde{I},\widetilde{J})
and
(\widetilde{I}^{\prime},\widetilde{J}^{\prime})
are in the same connected component, so are
(I,J)
and
(I^{\prime},J^{\prime})
.
∎

Remark 5.16
.

Let
I\subseteq E
be a subset of size
l\geqslant r
. We call
I
a
coindependent of rank
l
if
I^{c}
is an independent of the dual of
\mathfrak{M}
. Let
\mathcal{J}
be the set containing all the independents and all the coindependents. One can extend
\mathcal{G}
setting
\mathcal{V}:=\mathcal{J}\times\mathcal{J}
and defining the edges in a similar way to Definition
5.1
. Then Conjecture 1.8. of
[
16
]
is equivalent to the conjectural extension of Corollary
5.14
to
\mathcal{G}_{r,r}
. This conjecture is a part of the White’s conjecture about toric ideals of matroids.
∎

Now we arrive to the last section of this paper which generalizes Theorem 1.1 of
[
4
]
.

6.
Variation of Symanzik rational fractions

In this section, if
Y,Z\in\mathrm{\mathfrak{C}}^{k}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
are any two hypercubic matrices of order
k
and of size
n
, we set, for
k+1
families
u,u_{(1)},\dots,u_{(k)}
of size
p
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
,

(\mathbf{u}_{(1)},\dots,\mathbf{u}_{(k)})_{k,y}:=\mathrm{det}(Y\cdot_{1}U_{(1)}\cdots\cdot_{k}U_{(k)}),\qquad\lVert\mathbf{u}\rVert_{k,y}:=\sqrt[k]{(\mathbf{u},\dots,\mathbf{u})_{k,y}},

where, in this section,
(u,\dots,u)_{k,y}
will always be nonnegative.
Moreover, we use the same definition replacing
Y
by
Y+Z
and
y
by
y+z
. In the rest of the section, we will omit
k
from this notation. In particular,
\lVert{}\cdot{}\rVert_{y}:=\lVert{}\cdot{}\rVert_{k,y}
.

Let
n,p
be two positive integers, let
u
be a family of size
n
of elements of
\mathbb{R}^{p}
, and let
r
be its rank. We set
\mathfrak{M}:=\mathfrak{M}_{u}
the matroid associated to
u
. Let
v
be such that
v^{{}^{\intercal}}
is a basis of
\ker(U)
. Let
\beta\in\langle u\rangle
be any nonzero vector,
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
be such that
U\alpha=\beta
and
w:=(v^{{}^{\intercal}}\star(\alpha))^{{}^{\intercal}}
. Moreover, we set
\Delta
a simplicial complex of dimension
d>0
with
n
facets and
p

(d-1)
-dimensional faces.

In this last section, we state a nice property of Symanzik rational fractions defined in Definition
2.22
. One can roughly states it as
“a bounding deformation of the metric of a simplicial complex only implies a uniformly bounded variation of the Symanzik rational fraction with one parameter”
, where uniformly means that the bound does not depend on the chosen metric.

We can extend Propositions
2.16
and
2.19
to get

\mathrm{\widetilde{\mathrm{Sym}}}_{k}(u;(\beta);y)=\frac{\lVert\mathbf{w}^{{}^{\intercal}}\rVert_{y}^{k}}{\lVert\mathbf{v}^{{}^{\intercal}}\rVert_{y}^{k}}.

If
U
is the
d
-th incidence matrix of the simplicial complex
\Delta
of dimension
d
, we have seen that it is natural to assign the volume of the
i
-th facet of
\Delta
to
y_{i}
, for each
i\in[n]
. That is why we will deform the metric of
\Delta
by slightly perturbing
y
.

Let
k
be any even positive integer. Let
\mathcal{U}
be some space and
Z:\mathcal{U}\to\mathrm{\mathfrak{C}}^{k}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
be a bounded map (i.e., each entry is bounded).
Let
y_{1},\dots,y_{n}:\mathcal{U}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
be
n
functions and let

\begin{array}[]{rrcl}Y:&\mathcal{U}&\longrightarrow&\mathrm{\mathfrak{C}}^{k}_{n}(\mathbb{R}),\\
&t&\longmapsto&\mathrm{diag}^{k}(y_{1}(t),\dots,y_{n}(t)).\end{array}

Suppose that
(\mathbf{v},\dots,\mathbf{v})_{y(t)+z(t)}
is positive for all
t\in\mathcal{U}
. This is always true for sufficiently large
y_{1},\dots,y_{n}
.

If
\phi
and
\psi
are two functions from
\mathcal{U}
to
\mathbb{R}
, then the notation
\phi=\mathrm{\mathcal{O}}_{y}(\psi)
means that there exist two positive constants
c
and
C
such that, for all
t\in\mathcal{U}
,
y_{1}(t),\dots,y_{n}(t)\geqslant C
implies that
|\phi(t)|\leqslant c|\psi(t)|
. Similarly, the notation
\phi=\mathrm{o}_{y}(\psi)
means that, for all positive real
\varepsilon
, there exists a positive real
C_{\varepsilon}
such that, for all
t\in\mathcal{U}
,
y_{1}(t),\dots,y_{n}(t)\geqslant C_{\varepsilon}
implies that
|\phi(t)|\leqslant\varepsilon|\psi(t)|
.

Theorem 6.1
.

With the above notations, we have

\frac{\lVert\mathbf{w}^{{}^{\intercal}}\rVert_{y}^{k}}{\lVert\mathbf{v}^{{}^{\intercal}}\rVert_{y}^{k}}-\frac{\lVert\mathbf{w}^{{}^{\intercal}}\rVert_{y+z}^{k}}{\lVert\mathbf{v}^{{}^{\intercal}}\rVert_{y+z}^{k}}=\mathrm{\mathcal{O}}_{y}(1).

Much of the rest of this section is devoted to the proof of this theorem. The proof essentially follows that of Theorem 1.1 in
[
4
]
. Here are the main steps of the proof. First we find an equivalent statement where the left-hand member is the difference of two polynomials (Claim
6.2
, and beyond). Then we partially develop the polynomials in order to treat each term separately. Next, we introduce a graph
\mathfrak{G}
similar to the exchange graph
\mathcal{G}
of Section
5
. Each term corresponds to a vertex of
\mathfrak{G}
. Claim
6.4
states some properties of the terms which can be seen on the graph. For instance, if (the vertices corresponding to) two terms are linked, then the difference between them are negligible (more exactly, one has to multiply one of the term by an explicit constant for this to be true). Thus, if a term is negligible, all terms in its connected components are negligible. For other terms, the idea is to use Section
5
to associate bijectively each such term with another term in its connected component that compensates the first one (Claim
6.6
). Before that, Claim
6.5
explicits the link between
\mathfrak{G}
and
\mathcal{G}
.

Let us set the following functions

f_{1}:=\lVert\mathbf{v}^{{}^{\intercal}}\rVert_{y}^{k},\qquad f_{2}:=\lVert\mathbf{w}^{{}^{\intercal}}\rVert_{y}^{k},\qquad g_{1}:=\lVert\mathbf{v}^{{}^{\intercal}}\rVert_{y+z}^{k},\qquad g_{2}:=\lVert\mathbf{w}^{{}^{\intercal}}\rVert_{y+z}^{k}.

Let
e
be the canonical basis of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Decomposing along the standard orthonormal basis, we obtain

\displaystyle g_{1}

\displaystyle=\lVert\mathbf{v}^{{}^{\intercal}}\rVert_{y+z}^{k}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}K_{1},\dots,K_{k}\subseteq[n]\\
|K_{1}|=\dots=|K_{k}|=n-r\end{subarray}}(\mathbf{v}^{{}^{\intercal}},\mathbf{e}_{K_{1}})\cdots(\mathbf{v}^{{}^{\intercal}},\mathbf{e}_{K_{k}})(\mathbf{e}_{K_{1}},\dots,\mathbf{e}_{K_{k}})_{y+z}.

Remind Remark
4.6
about a condition for
\mathrm{det}(V_{K})
and
\mathrm{det}(W_{L})
to be nonzero. The previous formula can be rewritten in the form

(30)

g_{1}=\sum_{I_{1},\dots,I_{k}\in\mathfrak{I}_{r}}\mathrm{det}(V_{I_{1}^{c}})\cdots\mathrm{det}(V_{I_{k}^{c}})(\mathbf{e}_{I_{1}^{c}},\dots,\mathbf{e}_{I_{k}^{c}})_{y+z}.

In the same way,

\displaystyle g_{2}=\sum_{J_{1},\dots,J_{k}\in\mathfrak{I}_{r-1}}\mathrm{det}(W_{J_{1}^{c}})\cdots\mathrm{det}(W_{J_{k}^{c}})(\mathbf{e}_{J_{1}^{c}},\dots,\mathbf{e}_{J_{k}^{c}})_{y+z},

\displaystyle f_{1}=\sum_{I\in\mathfrak{I}_{r}}\mathrm{det}(V_{I^{c}})^{k}y^{I^{c}},

\displaystyle f_{2}=\sum_{J\in\mathfrak{I}_{r-1}}\mathrm{det}(W_{J^{c}})^{k}y^{J^{c}}.

Notice that
f_{1}
is a homogeneous polynomial of
\mathbb{R}[y]
of degree
n-r
and that all its coefficients are positive. Moreover all coefficients of
g_{1}
and
g_{2}
as polynomials of
\mathbb{R}[y]
are bounded.

If
I
is a subset of
[n]
and if
h\in\mathbb{R}[y]
, let us denote by
[y^{I}]h
the coefficient of the monomial
y^{I}
in
h
.
For example, if
I\in\mathfrak{I}_{r}
, the monomial
y^{I^{c}}
of
g_{1}
is only present in the term where all
I_{i}
s are equal to
I
. Thus

[y^{I^{c}}]g_{1}=[y^{I^{c}}]\Big(\mathrm{det}(V_{I^{c}})^{k}\lVert\mathbf{e}_{I^{c}}\rVert_{y+z}^{k}\Big)=\mathrm{det}(V_{I^{c}})^{k}.

We deduce that these coefficients are constant, and that for all
I\in\mathfrak{I}
,

(31)

[y^{I^{c}}]g_{1}=[y^{I^{c}}]f_{1}.

The statements of the theorem is that
f_{2}/f_{1}-g_{2}/g_{1}=\mathrm{\mathcal{O}}_{y}(1)
. Let us simplify this statement thanks to the following claim.

Claim 6.2
.

We have

g_{1}-f_{1}=\mathrm{o}_{y}(f_{1})
.

Proof of the claim.

By (
31
),
g_{1}-f_{1}
is a polynomial of degree at most
n-r-1
. Moreover, its coefficients are bounded functions. Let
K\subseteq[n]
be an arbitrary subset such that
[y^{K}](g_{1}-f_{1})
is nonzero. Then,
|K|<n-r
. Since
f_{1}
is homogeneous of degree
n-r
,
[y^{K}](g_{1}-f_{1})=[y^{K}]g_{1}
. From (
30
) we infer that
K\subseteq I_{1}^{c}\cap\dots\cap I_{k}^{c}
for some sets
I_{1},\dots,I_{k}\in\mathfrak{I}_{r}
. Thus,
K\subsetneq I_{1}^{c}
. It happens that
[y^{I_{1}^{c}}]f_{1}=\mathrm{det}(V_{I_{1}^{c}})^{k}
is a positive integer. Since
[y^{K}](g_{1}-f_{1})
is bounded,

\Big([y^{K}](g_{1}-f_{1})\Big)y^{K}=\mathrm{o}_{y}(y^{I_{1}^{c}}).

Since all coefficients of
f_{1}
are positive, we can sum all terms of
(g_{1}-f_{1})
, and then conclude the proof.
∎

Multiplying
f_{2}/f_{1}-g_{2}/g_{1}
by
f_{1}g_{1}
, and using the claim, it remains to show that

g_{1}f_{2}-f_{1}g_{2}=\mathrm{\mathcal{O}}_{y}(f_{1}^{2}).

Notice that the monomials with nonzero coefficients in
f_{1}^{2}
are exactly the monomials of the form
y^{I^{c}}y^{I^{\prime c}}
where
(I,I^{\prime})\in\mathfrak{I}_{r}\times\mathfrak{I}_{r}
.

Let us rewrite

g_{1}(t)f_{2}(t)=\sum_{I_{1},\dots,I_{k}\in\mathfrak{I}_{r}}\sum_{J\in\mathfrak{I}_{r-1}}s(I_{1},\dots,I_{k},J)h(I_{1},\dots,I_{k},J;t),

where

h(I_{1},\dots,I_{k},J;t):=(\mathbf{e}_{I_{1}^{c}},\dots,\mathbf{e}_{I_{k}^{c}})_{y+z}y^{J^{c}}

is a polynomial whose coefficients are functions, and

s(I_{1},\dots,I_{k},J)=\mathrm{det}(V_{I_{1}^{c}})\cdots\mathrm{det}(V_{I_{k}^{c}})\mathrm{det}(W_{J^{c}})^{k}

is a real number.
Similarly,

f_{1}(t)g_{2}(t)=\sum_{J_{1},\dots,J_{k}\in\mathfrak{I}_{r-1}}\sum_{I\in\mathfrak{I}_{r}}s(J_{1},\dots,J_{k},I)h(J_{1},\dots,J_{k},I;t),

where

\displaystyle h(J_{1},\dots,J_{k},I;t):=(\mathbf{e}_{J_{1}^{c}},\dots,\mathbf{e}_{J_{k}^{c}})_{y+z}y^{I^{c}},

\displaystyle s(J_{1},\dots,J_{k},I):=\mathrm{det}(W_{J_{1}^{c}})\cdots\mathrm{det}(W_{J_{k}^{c}})\mathrm{det}(V_{I^{c}})^{k}.

We will not develop the polynomials further.

It is clear that

\displaystyle h(K_{1},\dots,K_{k},L;t)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{\mathcal{O}}_{y}(y^{K^{c}_{1}\cap\dots\cap K^{c}_{n}}y^{L^{c}})

(32)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{\mathcal{O}}_{y}(y^{(K_{1}\cup\dots\cup K_{n})^{c}}y^{L^{c}}).

Let us define a new graph which is slightly similar to the exchange graph
\mathcal{G}_{r,r-1}
of
\mathfrak{M}
. Let
\mathfrak{G}=(\mathfrak{V},\mathfrak{E})
be a bipartite graph with vertex set
\mathfrak{V}=\mathfrak{V}_{r-1,r}\sqcup\mathfrak{V}_{r,r-1}
and edge set
\mathfrak{E}
where

\displaystyle\mathfrak{V}_{r-1,r}:=(\mathfrak{I}_{r-1})^{k}\times\mathfrak{I}_{r},

\displaystyle\mathfrak{V}_{r,r-1}:=(\mathfrak{I}_{r})^{k}\times\mathfrak{I}_{r-1},

and where two vertices
(J_{1},\dots,J_{k},I)\in\mathfrak{V}_{r-1,r}
and
(I_{1},\dots,I_{k},J)\in\mathfrak{V}_{r,r-1}
are connected by an edge if and only if there exists
i\in\mathrm{fr}(J_{1})\cap\dots\cap\mathrm{fr}(J_{k})\cap I
such that
I=J+i
and
I_{l}=J_{l}+i
, for all
l\in[k]
.

One can now see
h
and
s
as some functions on
\mathfrak{G}
. Moreover

\displaystyle g_{1}(t)f_{2}(t)=\sum_{\mathfrak{a}\in\mathfrak{V}_{r,r-1}}s(\mathfrak{a})h(\mathfrak{a};t),

\displaystyle g_{2}(t)f_{1}(t)=\sum_{\mathfrak{a}\in\mathfrak{V}_{r-1,r}}s(\mathfrak{a})h(\mathfrak{a};t).

Thus, each vertex of
\mathfrak{G}
corresponds to a part of the difference between both polynomials.

Let
\varepsilon
be define as in Lemma
2.15
. Let
\mathfrak{a}=(I_{1},\dots,I_{k},J)\in\mathfrak{V}_{r,r-1}
and
\mathfrak{b}=(J_{1},\dots,J_{k},I)
be two adjacent vertices of
\mathfrak{G}
. Let
i\in[n]
be such that
I=J+i
. We define

\eta_{\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b}}:=\eta_{\mathfrak{b},\mathfrak{a}}:=\varepsilon(I_{1}^{c}\star i)\cdots\varepsilon(I_{k}^{c}\star i),

where we omitted the brackets around
i
.

Definition 6.3
.

A vertex
(J_{1},\dots,J_{k},I)
in
\mathfrak{V}_{r-1,r}
is said
ordinary
if
\mathrm{fr}(J_{1})=\dots=\mathrm{fr}(J_{k})
. A vertex
(I_{1},\dots,I_{k},J)
in
\mathfrak{V}_{r,r-1}
is said
ordinary
if
I_{1}\cap\mathrm{fr}(J)=\dots=I_{k}\cap\mathrm{fr}(J)
. A vertex of
\mathfrak{V}
which is not ordinary is called
special
.
∎

Claim 6.4
.

Here are some properties of
h
and
s
.

(1)

If
\mathfrak{a}
and
\mathfrak{b}
are two adjacent vertices of
\mathfrak{G}
, then
h(\mathfrak{a};t)-\eta_{\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b}}h(\mathfrak{b};t)=\mathrm{\mathcal{O}}_{y}(f_{1}^{2})
.

(2)

If
\mathfrak{a}
is a special vertex of
\mathfrak{V}
, then
h(\mathfrak{a};t)=\mathrm{\mathcal{O}}_{y}(f_{1}^{2})
.

(3)

Let
\mathfrak{b}:=(J_{1},\dots,J_{k},I)\in\mathfrak{V}_{r-1,r}
be an ordinary vertex and let
\mathfrak{a}:=(I_{1},\dots,I_{k},J)\in\mathfrak{V}_{r,r-1}
be one of its neighbors. If
\mathrm{det}(W_{J_{1}^{c}})\neq 0
then, for all
\ell\in[k]
,

\frac{\mathrm{det}(V_{I_{\ell}^{c}})}{\mathrm{det}(V_{I_{1}^{c}})}=\frac{\varepsilon(I_{\ell}^{c}\star i)\mathrm{det}(W_{J_{\ell}^{c}})}{\varepsilon(I_{1}^{c}\star i)\mathrm{det}(W_{J_{1}^{c}})},

where
i
verifies
I=J+i
.

(4)

With the notations of the third point, if
s(\mathfrak{a})
and
s(\mathfrak{b})
are nonzero, then

\frac{s(\mathfrak{a})}{\mathrm{det}(V_{I^{c}_{1}})^{k}\mathrm{det}(W_{J^{c}})^{k}}=\eta_{\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b}}\frac{s(\mathfrak{b})}{\mathrm{det}(W_{J^{c}_{1}})^{k}\mathrm{det}(V_{I^{c}})^{k}}.

Proof.

(1)

Let
\mathfrak{a}=(J_{1},\dots,J_{k},I)\in\mathfrak{V}_{k-1,k}
and
\mathfrak{b}=(I_{1},\dots,I_{k},J)
be two adjacent vertices. Let
i\in[n]
be such that
I=J+i
. Let us extract
y_{i}
from
h(\mathfrak{a};t)
and from
h(\mathfrak{b};t)
. One has

\displaystyle h(I_{1},\dots,I_{k},J;t)

\displaystyle=(\mathbf{e}_{I_{1}^{c}},\dots,\mathbf{e}_{I_{k}^{c}})_{y+z}y^{J^{c}}

\displaystyle=\Big((\mathbf{e}_{I_{1}^{c}},\dots,\mathbf{e}_{I_{k}^{c}})_{y+z}y^{I^{c}}\Big)y_{i},

and, using the Laplace cofactor expansion along the column of the determinant which contains
y_{i}
,

\displaystyle h(J_{1},\dots,J_{k},I;t)

\displaystyle=(\mathbf{e}_{J_{1}^{c}},\dots,\mathbf{e}_{J_{k}^{c}})_{y+z}y^{I^{c}}

\displaystyle=\varepsilon(I_{1}^{c}\star i)\cdots\varepsilon(I_{k}^{c}\star i)(\mathbf{e}_{I_{1}^{c}}\wedge e_{i},\dots,\mathbf{e}_{I_{k}^{c}}\wedge e_{i})_{y+z}y^{I^{c}}

\displaystyle=\Big(\eta_{\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b}}(\mathbf{e}_{I_{1}^{c}},\dots,\mathbf{e}_{I_{k}^{c}})_{y+z}y_{i}+\mathrm{\mathcal{O}}_{y}(y^{I_{1}^{c}\cap\dots\cap I_{k}^{c}})\Big)y^{I^{c}}.

Thus,

\displaystyle h(\mathfrak{a};t)-\eta_{\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b}}h(\mathfrak{b};t)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{\mathcal{O}}_{y}(y^{I_{1}^{c}\cap\dots\cap I_{k}^{c}}y^{I^{c}})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{\mathcal{O}}_{y}(y^{I_{1}^{c}}y^{I^{c}}).

The monomial
y^{I_{1}^{c}}y^{I^{c}}
is present in
f_{1}^{2}
with a positive coefficient. Thus,

h(\mathfrak{a};t)-\eta_{\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b}}h(\mathfrak{b};t)=\mathrm{\mathcal{O}}_{y}(f_{1}^{2}).

(2)

Since there are two kinds of special vertices, we will make two cases.

Let
\mathfrak{a}=(J_{1},\dots,J_{k},I)
be a special vertex of
\mathfrak{V}_{r-1,r}
. Assume, without loss of generality, that
\mathrm{fr}(J_{1})\neq\mathrm{fr}(J_{2})
. We have seen in Equation (
32
) that

h(\mathfrak{a};t)=\mathrm{\mathcal{O}}_{y}(y^{(J_{1}\cup\dots\cup J_{k})^{c}}y^{I^{c}}).

By Lemma
5.12
,
\mathrm{fr}(J_{1})\neq\mathrm{fr}(J_{2})
implies that there exists
j\in J_{1}\cap\mathrm{fr}(J_{2})
. Since
\mathrm{rk}(J_{2})=r-1
, the set
I^{\prime}:=J_{2}+j
is in
\mathfrak{I}_{r}
. But
I^{\prime}\subseteq J_{1}\cup\dots\cup J_{k}
, and so

y^{(J_{1}\cup\dots\cup J_{k})^{c}}=\mathrm{\mathcal{O}}_{y}(y^{I^{\prime c}}).

Then,

h(\mathfrak{a};t)=\mathrm{\mathcal{O}}_{y}(y^{I^{\prime c}}y^{I^{c}})=\mathrm{\mathcal{O}}_{y}(f_{1}^{2}).

In the same way, if
\mathfrak{a}=(I_{1},\dots,I_{k},J)
is a special vertex of
\mathfrak{V}_{r,r-1}
, we have

h(\mathfrak{a};t)=\mathrm{\mathcal{O}}_{y}(y^{(I_{1}\cup\dots\cup I_{k})^{c}}y^{J^{c}}).

Assume, without loss of generality, that there exists an element
i
in
(I_{1}\cap\mathrm{fr}(J))\setminus(I_{2}\cap\mathrm{fr}(J))
. One has

i\not\in(I_{1}\cup\dots\cup I_{k})^{c},\qquad(I_{1}\cup\dots\cup I_{k})^{c}\subseteq I_{2}^{c},\qquad i\in I_{2}^{c}.

This implies
(I_{1}\cup\dots\cup I_{k})^{c}+i\subseteq I_{2}^{c}
. Moreover, the set
I:=J+i
is in
\mathfrak{I}_{r}
. One obtains

\displaystyle y^{(I_{1}\cup\dots\cup I_{k})^{c}}y^{J^{c}}

\displaystyle=y^{(I_{1}\cup\dots\cup I_{k})^{c}+i}y^{J^{c}-i}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{\mathcal{O}}_{y}(y^{I_{2}^{c}}y^{I^{c}}).

Finally,

h(\mathfrak{a};t)=\mathrm{\mathcal{O}}_{y}(f_{1}^{2}).

(3)

Let
\widetilde{w}:=w\star((0,\dots,0,-1))
and
\widetilde{u}
such that
\widetilde{u}^{{}^{\intercal}}
is a basis of
\langle(u\star(\beta))^{{}^{\intercal}}\rangle
. Notice that
\widetilde{U}{\widetilde{W}}^{{}^{\intercal}}=0
. The statement is equivalent to

\frac{\mathrm{det}(\widetilde{W}_{I_{\ell}^{c}+(n+1)})}{\mathrm{det}(\widetilde{W}_{I_{1}^{c}+(n+1)})}=\frac{\varepsilon(I_{\ell}^{c}\star i)\mathrm{det}(\widetilde{W}_{J_{\ell}^{c}})}{\varepsilon(I_{1}^{c}\star i)\mathrm{det}(\widetilde{W}_{J_{1}^{c}})}.

The families
\widetilde{u}
and
\widetilde{w}
verify the conditions of Lemma
2.15
. Applying the lemma, last equation is equivalent to

(33)

\frac{\varepsilon((I_{\ell}^{c}+(n+1))\star I_{\ell})\mathrm{det}(\widetilde{U}_{I_{\ell}})}{\varepsilon((I_{1}^{c}+(n+1))\star I_{1})\mathrm{det}(\widetilde{U}_{I_{1}})}=\frac{\varepsilon(I_{\ell}^{c}\star i)\varepsilon(J_{\ell}^{c}\star(J_{\ell}+(n+1)))\mathrm{det}(\widetilde{U}_{J_{\ell}+(n+1)})}{\varepsilon(I_{1}^{c}\star i)\varepsilon(J_{1}^{c}\star(J_{1}+(n+1)))\mathrm{det}(\widetilde{U}_{J_{1}+(n+1)})}.

We can make some simplifications. For example,

\varepsilon((I_{\ell}^{c}+(n+1))\star I_{\ell})=(-1)^{|I_{\ell}|}\varepsilon(I_{\ell}^{c}\star I_{\ell}\star(n+1))=(-1)^{|I_{\ell}|}\varepsilon(I_{\ell}^{c}\star I_{\ell}).

After simplification, we get

\frac{\varepsilon(I_{\ell}^{c}\star I_{\ell})\varepsilon(J_{\ell}\star i)\mathrm{det}(\widetilde{U}_{J_{\ell}}\star\widetilde{U}_{i})}{\varepsilon(I_{1}^{c}\star I_{1})\varepsilon(J_{1}\star i)\mathrm{det}(\widetilde{U}_{J_{1}}\star\widetilde{U}_{i})}=\frac{\varepsilon(I_{\ell}^{c}\star i\star J_{\ell})\mathrm{det}(\widetilde{U}_{J_{\ell}+(n+1)})}{\varepsilon(J_{1}^{c}\star i\star J_{1})\mathrm{det}(\widetilde{U}_{J_{1}+(n+1)})}.

As
\mathfrak{b}
is ordinary,
\mathrm{cl}(J_{\ell})=\mathrm{cl}(J_{1})
. Thus there exists
P\in\mathcal{M}_{r-1}(\mathbb{R})
such that
U_{J_{\ell}}=U_{J_{1}}P
. Hence, setting

\widetilde{P}=\left(\!\raisebox{-0.4pt}{\hbox to46.97pt{\vbox to43.99pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-13.45404pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\!\right),

one obtains
\widetilde{U}_{J_{\ell}}\star\widetilde{U}_{i}=(\widetilde{U}_{J_{1}}\star\widetilde{U}_{i})\widetilde{P}
and
\widetilde{U}_{J_{\ell}+(n+1)}=\widetilde{U}_{J_{1}+(n+1)}\widetilde{P}
. So, both ratios equal
\mathrm{det}(P)
up to a sign.

We now compute the sign of the numerators:

\displaystyle\varepsilon(I_{\ell}^{c}\star I_{\ell})\varepsilon(J_{\ell}\star i)\varepsilon(I_{\ell}^{c}\star i\star J_{\ell})

\displaystyle=\varepsilon(I_{\ell}^{c}\star i\star J_{\ell})\varepsilon(i\star J_{\ell})\varepsilon(J_{\ell}\star i)\varepsilon(I_{\ell}^{c}\star i\star J_{\ell})

\displaystyle=(-1)^{r-1}.

The signs of the numerators and the denominators simplify, which concludes the proof.

(4)

Since
s(\mathfrak{a})
and
s(\mathfrak{b})
are nonzero,
\mathrm{det}(W_{J^{c}})
and
\mathrm{det}(W_{J_{1}^{c}})
are nonzero too. Thus we can apply Point (
3
) for all
\ell\in[k]
. Multiplying left-hand members and right-hand members, we get the equality of Point (
4
). ∎

Let
\mathrm{CC}(\mathfrak{G})
be the set of connected components of
\mathfrak{G}
. If
\mathfrak{H}=(\mathfrak{V}^{\prime},\mathfrak{E}^{\prime})\in\mathrm{CC}(\mathfrak{G})
, we set

\displaystyle\phantom{\text{ and }}\mathfrak{V}^{\prime}_{r-1,r}:=\mathfrak{V}^{\prime}\cap\mathfrak{V}_{r-1,r}\text{ and }

\displaystyle\mathfrak{V}^{\prime}_{r,r-1}:=\mathfrak{V}^{\prime}\cap\mathfrak{V}_{r,r-1}.

Moreover, we denote by
\mathrm{OCC}(\mathfrak{G})
the set of
ordinary connected components
of
\mathfrak{G}
that only contain ordinary vertices. Let
\mathrm{SCC}(\mathfrak{G}):=\mathrm{CC}(\mathfrak{G})\setminus\mathrm{OCC}(\mathfrak{G})
be the set of
special connected components
of
\mathfrak{G}
.

The equation we wanted to show, namely,

g_{1}f_{2}-g_{2}f_{1}=\mathrm{\mathcal{O}}_{y}(f_{1}^{2}),

is equivalent to

\sum_{(\mathfrak{V}^{\prime},\mathfrak{E}^{\prime})\in\mathrm{CC}(\mathfrak{G})}\bigg(\sum_{\mathfrak{a}\in\mathfrak{V}^{\prime}_{r,r-1}}s(\mathfrak{a})h(\mathfrak{a};t)-\sum_{\mathfrak{a}\in\mathfrak{V}^{\prime}_{r-1,r}}s(\mathfrak{a})h(\mathfrak{a};t)\bigg)=\mathrm{\mathcal{O}}_{y}(f_{1}^{2}).

In the above sum, we can remove the special connected components because of Points (
2
) and (
1
) of Claim
6.4
. Thus, it remains to show

\sum_{(\mathfrak{V}^{\prime},\mathfrak{E}^{\prime})\in\mathrm{OCC}(\mathfrak{G})}\bigg(\sum_{\mathfrak{a}\in\mathfrak{V}^{\prime}_{r,r-1}}s(\mathfrak{a})h(\mathfrak{a};t)-\sum_{\mathfrak{a}\in\mathfrak{V}^{\prime}_{r-1,r}}s(\mathfrak{a})h(\mathfrak{a};t)\bigg)=\mathrm{\mathcal{O}}_{y}(f_{1}^{2}).

Actually, we will prove that, for all
\mathfrak{H}=(\mathfrak{V}^{\prime},\mathfrak{E}^{\prime})\in\mathrm{OCC}(\mathfrak{G})
,

(34)

\sum_{\mathfrak{a}\in\mathfrak{V}^{\prime}_{r,r-1}}s(\mathfrak{a})h(\mathfrak{a};t)-\sum_{\mathfrak{a}\in\mathfrak{V}^{\prime}_{r-1,r}}s(\mathfrak{a})h(\mathfrak{a};t)=\mathrm{\mathcal{O}}_{y}(f_{1}^{2}).

We now see that any ordinary connected component of
\mathfrak{G}
is naturally isomorphic to a connected component of (a subgraph of) the exchange graph of
\mathfrak{M}
. This will allow us to applied the results of Section
5
. More precisely, for each
\mathfrak{a}\in\mathfrak{V}^{\prime}_{r,r-1}
, we will find
\mathfrak{b}\in\mathfrak{V}^{\prime}_{r-1,r}
in the same connected component such that

s(\mathfrak{a})h(\mathfrak{a};t)-s(\mathfrak{b})h(\mathfrak{b};t)=\mathrm{\mathcal{O}}_{y}(f_{1}^{2}).

Fix a connected component
\mathfrak{H}=(\mathfrak{V}^{\prime},\mathfrak{E}^{\prime})
in
\mathrm{OCC}(\mathfrak{G})
. Let
\mathcal{G}=(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E})
be the exchange graph of
\mathfrak{M}
, as defined in Section
5
. We define the following projection:

\begin{array}[]{rrcl}\pi:&\mathfrak{V}&\to&\mathcal{V}_{r,r-1}\sqcup\mathcal{V}_{r-1,r},\\
&(K_{1},\dots,K_{k},L)&\mapsto&(K_{1},L).\end{array}

Let
\mathcal{H}:=\mathcal{G}[\pi(\mathfrak{V}^{\prime})]
be the induced subgraph of
\mathcal{G}
with vertex set the image of
\mathfrak{V}^{\prime}
by
\pi
. Let
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}
be its vertex set and
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
be its edge set. We have the following claim.

Claim 6.5
.

\mathcal{H}
is a connected component of
\mathcal{G}_{r,r-1}
, and the map
\pi
induces an isomorphism of graphs between
\mathfrak{H}
and
\mathcal{H}
.

Proof.

First we prove that
\pi
is injective on
\mathfrak{H}
. Notice that, as for
\mathcal{G}
, for every
l\in[k]
,
K_{l}\uplus L
is invariant in the connected components of
\mathfrak{G}
, i.e., if
\mathfrak{a}=(K_{1},\dots,K_{k},L)
and
\mathfrak{b}=(K^{\prime}_{1},\dots,K^{\prime}_{k},L^{\prime})
are two vertices in
\mathfrak{H}
, then
K_{l}\uplus L=K^{\prime}_{l}\uplus L^{\prime}
for all
l\in[k]
. Thus, if we know
\mathfrak{a}
, we can retrieves
\mathfrak{b}
only knowing
L^{\prime}
. But
L^{\prime}
is encoded in
\pi(\mathfrak{b})
. That concludes the injectivity. Thus
\pi
induces a bijection between
\mathfrak{V}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}
.

Next, we prove that
\pi
induces a natural bijection between
\mathfrak{E}^{\prime}
and edges of
\mathcal{G}_{r,r-1}
which are incident to a vertex of
\mathcal{H}
. Let
\mathfrak{e}\in\mathfrak{E}^{\prime}
and let
\mathfrak{a}
and
\mathfrak{b}
be its two endpoints. Using the definitions, it is clear that
\pi(\mathfrak{a})
and
\pi(\mathfrak{b})
are linked.

Reciprocally, let
e
be an edge of
\mathcal{G}_{r,r-1}
which is incident to a vertex
a
of
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}
. Let
\mathfrak{a}\in\mathfrak{V}^{\prime}
such that
\pi(\mathfrak{a})=a
. Let
b
be the other endpoint of
e
. We want to show that there exists
\mathfrak{b}
in
\mathfrak{V}
such that
\pi(\mathfrak{b})=b
and that
\mathfrak{a}
and
\mathfrak{b}
are connected by an edge. There are two cases.

•

If
\mathfrak{a}=(J_{1},\dots,J_{k},I)\in\mathfrak{V}_{r-1,r}
, then
a=(J_{1},I)
. There exists
i\in\mathrm{fr}(J_{1})
such that
b=(J_{1}+i,I-i)
. Since
\mathfrak{a}
is an ordinary vertex,
i\in\mathrm{fr}(J_{1})
implies that
i\in\mathrm{fr}(J_{l})
for all
l\in[k]
. Thus,
\mathfrak{b}:=(J_{1}+i,\dots,J_{r}+i,I-i)
is a neighbor of
\mathfrak{a}
such that
\pi(\mathfrak{b})=b
.

•

If
\mathfrak{a}=(I_{1},\dots,I_{k},J)\in\mathfrak{V}_{r,r-1}
, then
a=(I_{1},J)
. There exists
i\in\mathrm{fr}(J)\cap I_{1}
such that
b=(I_{1}-i,J+i)
. Since
\mathfrak{a}
is a special vertex,
i\in\mathrm{fr}(J)\cap I_{l}
for all
l\in[k]
. Finally,
\mathfrak{b}:=(I_{1}-i,\dots,I_{k}-i,J+i)
is a neighbor of
\mathfrak{a}
in
\mathfrak{H}
, and
\pi(\mathfrak{b})=b
.

We have proved that
\pi
induces a natural bijection between vertices of
\mathfrak{H}
and vertices of
\mathcal{H}
, and between edges of
\mathfrak{H}
and edges of
\mathcal{G}_{r,r-1}
which are incident to a vertex of
\mathcal{H}
. Thus, the claim is true.
∎

Let us denote by
\pi^{\prime}:\mathfrak{H}\to\mathcal{H}
the isomorphism induced by
\pi
.

We now study a second bijection. The well-definiteness will be justified in Claim
6.6
below. Set

\begin{array}[]{rrcl}\Phi:&\mathcal{V}^{\prime}_{r,r-1}&\to&\mathcal{V}^{\prime}_{r-1,r},\\
&(I,J)&\mapsto&\Big(A\cup(J\setminus B),B\cup(I\setminus A)\Big),\end{array}

where
(A,B)
is the MCP of any vertex of
\mathcal{H}
.
This map induces a map on
\mathfrak{H}
:

\begin{array}[]{rrcl}\widetilde{\Phi}:&\mathfrak{V}^{\prime}_{r,r-1}&\to&\mathfrak{V}^{\prime}_{r-1,r},\\
&\mathfrak{a}&\mapsto&\pi^{\prime-1}\circ\Phi\circ\pi^{\prime}(\mathfrak{a}).\end{array}

Claim 6.6
.

\Phi
and
\widetilde{\Phi}
have the following properties.

(1)

\Phi
and
\widetilde{\Phi}
are well-defined, and both are bijections.

(2)

If
\mathfrak{a}\in\mathfrak{V}^{\prime}_{r,r-1}
, then
s(\mathfrak{a})h(\mathfrak{a};t)-s(\widetilde{\Phi}(\mathfrak{a}))h(\widetilde{\Phi}(\mathfrak{a});t)=\mathrm{\mathcal{O}}_{y}(f_{1}^{2})
.

Proof.

We prove the two points independently.

(1)

It is enough to show the first point for
\Phi
. Let
(I,J)\in\mathcal{V}^{\prime}_{r,r-1}
. Let
(A,B):=\mathrm{MCP}(I,J)
. Let
(J^{\prime},I^{\prime}):=\Big(A\cup(J\setminus B),B\cup(I\setminus A)\Big)
. We want to apply Corollary
5.14
in order to show that
(J^{\prime},I^{\prime})\in\mathcal{V}^{\prime}
. In the definition of
(J^{\prime},I^{\prime})
, the unions are disjoint. Indeed, if
i\in A\cap J
, resp.
i\in B\cap I
, then
(\{i\},\{i\})
is a codependent pair of
(I,J)
, and so
i\in B
, resp.
i\in A
. Thus

(A\sqcup(J\setminus B))\uplus(B\sqcup(I\setminus A))=I\uplus J.

Set
(A^{\prime},B^{\prime}):=\mathrm{MCP}(J^{\prime},I^{\prime})
. Clearly
(A,B)\subseteq(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})
. Moreover

\displaystyle\mathrm{cl}(A^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{cl}(A\sqcup(A^{\prime}\setminus A))

\displaystyle=\mathrm{cl}(\mathrm{cl}(A)\sqcup(A^{\prime}\setminus A))

\displaystyle=\mathrm{cl}(\mathrm{cl}(B)\sqcup(A^{\prime}\setminus A))

\displaystyle=\mathrm{cl}(B\sqcup(A^{\prime}\setminus A)).

Similarly,

\mathrm{cl}(B^{\prime})=\mathrm{cl}(A\sqcup(B^{\prime}\setminus B)).

But
B\sqcup(A^{\prime}\setminus A)\subseteq B\sqcup(J^{\prime}\setminus A)=J
and
A\sqcup(B^{\prime}\setminus B)\subseteq I
. Since

\mathrm{cl}(A\sqcup(B^{\prime}\setminus B))=\mathrm{cl}(B^{\prime})=\mathrm{cl}(A^{\prime})=\mathrm{cl}(B\sqcup(A^{\prime}\setminus A)),

\Big(A\sqcup(B^{\prime}\setminus B),B\sqcup(A^{\prime}\setminus A)\Big)
is a codependent pair of
(I,J)
. Thus, it is included in
(A,B)
, and so
A^{\prime}\setminus A
and
B^{\prime}\setminus B
are empty. Finally,

I\uplus J=J^{\prime}\uplus I^{\prime}\text{ and }\mathrm{MCP}(I,J)=\mathrm{MCP}(J^{\prime},I^{\prime}).

We can apply Corollary
5.14
, and we obtain
(J^{\prime},I^{\prime})\in\mathcal{V}^{\prime}_{r-1,r}
. Thus,
\Phi
is well-defined.

One can easily retrieve
(I,J)
from
(J^{\prime},I^{\prime})
by
(I,J):=(A\cup(I^{\prime}\setminus B),B\cup(J^{\prime}\setminus A))
. Thus,
\Phi
is a bijection. So is
\widetilde{\Phi}
.

(2)

Let
\mathfrak{a}=(I,I_{2},\dots,I_{k},J)\in\mathfrak{V}_{r,r-1}
be a vertex and let
\mathfrak{b}=(J^{\prime},J_{2},\dots,J_{k},I^{\prime})\in\mathfrak{V}_{r-1,r}
be the image of
\mathfrak{a}
by
\widetilde{\Phi}
. Let
(\mathfrak{a}=\mathfrak{a}_{0},\mathfrak{a}_{1},\dots,\mathfrak{a}_{m}=\mathfrak{b})
be a path from
\mathfrak{a}
to
\mathfrak{b}
. Point (
1
) of Claim
6.4
used
m
times show that

\displaystyle h(\mathfrak{a};t)-\eta_{\mathfrak{a}_{0},\mathfrak{a}_{1}}\cdots\eta_{\mathfrak{a}_{m-1},\mathfrak{a}_{m}}h(\mathfrak{b};t)=\mathrm{\mathcal{O}}_{y}(f_{1}^{2}).

It remains to prove that

(35)

s(\mathfrak{a})=\eta_{\mathfrak{a}_{0},\mathfrak{a}_{1}}\cdots\eta_{\mathfrak{a}_{m-1},\mathfrak{a}_{m}}s(\mathfrak{b}).

We can assume without loss of generality that
s(\mathfrak{a}_{0}),\dots,s(\mathfrak{a}_{m})
are nonzero. Indeed, this is true for all
\beta
belonging to a dense subset of
\langle u\rangle
. Moreover, both members of Equation (
35
) are continuous in
\beta
.

Point (
4
) of Claim
6.4
used
m
times show that

\displaystyle\frac{s(\mathfrak{a})}{\mathrm{det}(V_{I^{c}})^{k}\mathrm{det}(W_{J^{c}})^{k}}=\eta_{\mathfrak{a}_{0},\mathfrak{a}_{1}}\cdots\eta_{\mathfrak{a}_{m-1},\mathfrak{a}_{m}}\frac{s(\mathfrak{b})}{\mathrm{det}(W_{J^{\prime c}})^{k}\mathrm{det}(V_{I^{\prime c}})^{k}}.

It remains to show that
\mathrm{det}(V_{I^{c}})^{k}\mathrm{det}(W_{J^{c}})^{k}=\mathrm{det}(V_{I^{\prime c}})^{k}\mathrm{det}(W_{J^{\prime c}})^{k}
, i.e., that

\left|\frac{\mathrm{det}(V_{I^{\prime c}})}{\mathrm{det}(V_{I^{c}})}\right|=\left|\frac{\mathrm{det}(W_{J^{c}})}{\mathrm{det}(W_{J^{\prime c}})}\right|.

The proof will be very similar to the proof of (
3
) in Claim
6.4
. We use the same notations. It is enough to show that the analogous of Equation (
33
) holds. Since we do not care about signs, this analogous is

(36)

\left|\frac{\mathrm{det}(\widetilde{U}_{I^{\prime}})}{\mathrm{det}(\widetilde{U}_{I})}\right|=\left|\frac{\mathrm{det}(\widetilde{U}_{J+(n+1)})}{\mathrm{det}(\widetilde{U}_{J^{\prime}+(n+1)})}\right|.

Set
(A,B):=\mathrm{MCP}(I,J)
. From
\mathrm{cl}(A)=\mathrm{cl}(B)
we deduce that there exists a (unique) matrix
\widetilde{P}\in\mathcal{M}_{r}(\mathbb{R})
, of the form

\left(\,\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\hbox to38.24pt{\vbox to38.24pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-18.1254pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{

\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope }}}
